Commutator Estimates and Quantitative Local Weyl’s Law for Schrödinger Operators with Non-Smooth Potentials
Esteban Cárdenas, Laurent Lafleche

Abstract
. We analyze semiclassical Schrödinger operators with potentials of class C 1 , 1 / 2 C^{1,1/2} and establish commutator estimates for the associated projection operators in Schatten norms. These are then applied to prove quantitative versions of the local and phase space Weyl laws in L p L^{p} spaces. We study both non-interacting, and interacting particle systems. In particular, we are able to treat the case of the minimizers of the Hartree energy in the case of repulsive singular pair interactions such as the Coulomb potential.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
35J10
\cdot
81S30
\cdot
47B47 (47B15, 47B10, 35P30).

1.
Introduction

In this article, we study several asymptotic results related to the spectral projection

{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}:=\mathds{1}_{H\leq 0}

on the eigenspace of the negative eigenvalues of the Schrödinger operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

(1)

H=-\hbar^{2}\Delta+V(x)

where
V:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a function playing the role of the potential energy, identified with an operator of multiplication by
V
. Here,
\mathds{1}_{H\leq 0}=\mathds{1}_{(-\infty,0]}(H)
is defined via functional calculus. We analyze the so-called
semiclassical limit
in which the Planck constant

h=2\pi\hbar

converges to
0
. In particular, we are interested in the case when the potential
V
is not smooth but of class
C^{1,\alpha}
. Such potentials naturally arise in effective one-body problems of particle systems interacting via mean-field forces, which are induced by singular pair potentials, such as the Coulomb potential.

1.1.
Weyl’s laws

A fundamental result in the semiclassical limit of Schrödinger operators is Weyl’s law, which states that as
\hbar\to 0

(2)

N:=\tr\!\left(\mathds{1}_{H\leq 0}\right)=h^{-d}\int_{\left|{\xi}\right|^{2}+V(x)\leq 0}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi+o(h^{-d})\,,

see e.g.
[
53
, Theorem A.2.1]
. In particular, much activity has been devoted to understanding the rate of convergence in terms of
\hbar
, see e.g.
[
32
]
, and
[
51
]
, where the optimal rate was obtained for smooth potentials. For non-smooth potentials, it was recently proved in
[
47
]
that for potentials in the class
C^{1,\alpha}
for
\alpha\geq\frac{1}{2}
and
d\geq 3
, it holds for all
\hbar\in(0,1)
that

(3)

\left|{Nh^{d}-\int_{\left|{\xi}\right|^{2}+V(x)\leq 0}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p}\right|\leq\mathcal{C}_{0}\,\hbar

for a constant
\mathcal{C}_{0}>0
depending only on
V
. The rate of convergence in (
3
) is optimal, and its implications are crucial in the rest of the article. Indeed, in our setting it implies the validity of the
local eigenvalue estimate

(4)

h^{d}\tr\mathds{1}_{[a,b]}(H)\leq\mathcal{C}_{1}\left(\left|{b-a}\right|+\hbar\right)

for all appropriate values of
a<b
around zero. Here again
\mathcal{C}_{1}>0
is a distinguished constant, independent of
\hbar
.

While Weyl’s law describes the total number of negative eigenvalues of the Schrödinger operator
H=-\hbar^{2}\Delta+V
, one may also wonder about the average space distribution of its eigenfunctions. To be more precise, if
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
is a density operator, i.e. a non-negative trace-class operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, one can introduce its position density
1
1

1

The position density can be more generally defined in the weak sense by the formula
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\,\varphi=h^{d}\tr\!\left({\boldsymbol{\rho}}\,\varphi\right)
for any
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

(5)

\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}(x):=h^{d}{\boldsymbol{\rho}}(x,x)\,,

where
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}(x,y)
denotes the integral kernel of the operator
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
, and study its limit. When
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}={\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
, it is known under general assumptions that the following limit is true, and is known in the literature as the
local Weyl law
(see e.g.
[
24
]
)

(6)

\lim_{\hbar\to 0^{+}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\omega_{d}\,V_{-}^{d/2},

where
\omega_{d}=\frac{\pi^{d/2}}{\Gamma(1+d/2)}
is the volume of the unit ball in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Again, for smooth potentials, one can obtain rates of convergence in weak norms, see e.g.
[
14
,
34
,
55
,
20
]
.

These results can be seen as rigorous examples of the quantum-classical correspondence. More generally, one can consider the localization in phase space of the above limit, which is sometimes called convergence of states
[
21
]
. For this purpose, one can introduce the Wigner transform of the density operator
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
, which is the function of the phase space defined by

(7)

f_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}(x,\xi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-i\,y\cdot\xi/\hbar}\,{\boldsymbol{\rho}}(x+\tfrac{y}{2},x-\tfrac{y}{2})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y

for
(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
\xi
represents the momentum. This is the inverse operation of the Weyl quantization, which to a real-valued phase space density function
f
associates a self-adjoint operator
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{f}
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with integral kernel

(8)

{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{f}(x,y)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-2i\pi\left(x-y\right)\cdot\xi}\,f\big(\tfrac{x+y}{2},h\xi\big)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi\,.

The
phase space Weyl law
then tells that

(9)

\lim_{\hbar\to 0^{+}}f_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\mathds{1}_{\left|{\xi}\right|^{2}+V(x)\leq 0}\,.

This limit has been studied in the physics literature
[
4
,
18
,
19
]
.

One may observe that each limit is in a sense stronger than the previous one due to the following relations

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi=\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\quad\text{ and }\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}=h^{d}\tr\!\left({\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right),

for any sufficiently nice density operator
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
, and the fact that
\varrho_{f}=\omega_{d}\,V_{-}^{d/2}
, where, in the classical case, the position density of a phase space distribution
f:\mathbb{R}^{2d}\to\mathbb{R}
is defined by

(10)

\varrho_{f}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x,\xi)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi\,.

One of the questions that we are interested in this article is the convergence rate of the limits (
6
) and (
9
).

1.2.
Commutator estimates and semiclassical regularity

It is not surprising that in order to understand the rate at which the limits (
6
) and (
9
) hold, it is useful to understand the regularity properties of the functions
\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
and
f_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
that are
uniform in
\hbar
. In connection with this question is the relatively recent need for commutator estimates of the form

(11)

h^{d}\tr\!\left(\left|{\left[{x,{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\right]}\right|^{p}\right)\leq C\,\hbar\quad\text{ and }\quad h^{d}\tr\!\left(\left|{\left[{\hbar\nabla,{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\right]}\right|^{p}\right)\leq C\,\hbar

for
1\leq p<\infty
, with
C
independent of
\hbar
. To the authors’ knowledge, such estimates appeared first for
p=1
, as requirements on the initial data in the quantitative derivation of the Hartree–Fock equation from dynamics of fermions
[
10
]
. Here,
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
is a rank
N
orthogonal projection on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, corresponding to the reduced one-particle density matrix of a Slater determinant in semiclassical scaling
\hbar=N^{-1/d}
. Since then, these estimates have appeared multiple times in the literature. For instance, in
[
9
]
the authors extended their results to fermions with relativistic dispersion, whereas in
[
7
]
they extended them to the dynamics of mixed quasi-free states. Similar variants have been used in
[
25
]
to study extended system of fermions, and in
[
13
]
to study the dynamics of Bose–Fermi mixtures. The estimates for
p=2
, on the other hand, were employed in
[
46
]
to study the dynamics of fermions with non-zero pairing, and also in
[
12
]
to understand the strong convergence of ground states of
N
-body problems. In
[
37
]
, it is proved that they also give a bound on the self-distance in the quantum Wasserstein pseudo-metrics introduced in
[
27
,
28
]
, which allows to understand the rate of convergence in the mean-field and semiclassical limits in these two works, as well as in other works using these pseudo-metrics such as
[
36
,
13
,
15
]
.

As mentioned above, the estimates (
11
) are usually imposed on the initial datum. In practice, verifying such estimates for Slater determinants is not an easy task. The first example was given in
[
10
]
for the free Fermi gas on the torus. Later, the authors of
[
23
]
proved their validity in the case
p=1
for Schrödinger operators
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\mathds{1}_{H\leq 0}
, with
V
is smooth, see also
[
20
]
for similar estimates when
p=2
. The techniques used in
[
23
]
rely on the use of pseudo-differential operator techniques and, in particular, knowledge of the optimal Weyl law (
3
). Additionally, for the harmonic oscillator
[
6
,
39
]
the estimates were verified directly using creation and annihilation operator calculus. While we also rely on the validity of (
3
), our proof does not use additional pseudo-differential operator techniques. In particular, with our methods we are able to study the validity of (
11
) for the case
V\in C^{1,\alpha}_{\rm loc}
with
\alpha\geq\frac{1}{2}
, and for the ground state of an interacting Fermi system in the Hartree approximation with singular interactions.

The link between commutators estimates and uniform-in-
\hbar
regularity follows from the correspondence principle, which tells us that the quantum analogue of the gradients in the phase space should be

(12)

\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}{\boldsymbol{\rho}}:=\left[{\nabla,{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\right]\quad\text{ and }\quad\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}{\boldsymbol{\rho}}:=\frac{1}{i\hbar}\left[{x,{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\right]

and indeed, these correspond to taking the gradient of the Wigner transform in the sense that one verifies that

\nabla_{x}f_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}=f_{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\quad\text{ and }\quad\nabla_{\xi}f_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}=f_{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\,.

In particular,
\nabla\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}=\varrho_{\,\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}
, and the commutator estimates (
11
) with
p=1
imply uniform-in-
\hbar

W^{1,1}
regularity for the position density
\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
, that is
\left\|{\nabla\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\right\|_{L^{1}}
is bounded independently of
\hbar
. In terms of phase space regularity, one can first notice that the analogue of the phase space integral of a density is given by
h^{d}\,\tr
, which justifies the use of scaled Schatten norms for
p\geq 1
,

(13)

\left\|{{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{p}}:=\left(h^{d}\tr\!\left(\left|{{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\right|^{p}\right)\right)^{1/p},

with
\left\|{{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}
denoting the operator norm. The Wigner transform is then an isomorphism from
\mathcal{L}^{2}
to
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
, that is
\left\|{f_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\right\|_{L^{2}}=\left\|{{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}
. We infer from this that the commutator estimates (
11
) with
p=2
imply bounds of the form
\left\|{\nabla f_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\right\|_{L^{2}}\leq C/\sqrt{\hbar}
. The fact that
f_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
is not in
H^{1}
uniformly in
\hbar
should not come as a surprise when
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}=\mathds{1}_{H\leq 0}
, since the phase space Weyl law (
9
) states that
f_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
converges to a characteristic function of the phase space. It is however proved in
[
39
]
that the commutator estimates (
11
) with
p=1
implies that
f_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
uniformly in
\hbar
for any
s<1/2
, and that this regularity order is optimal.

1.3.
Interacting particles

In this article, we consider the
Hartree
energy functional (sometimes also called reduced Hartree–Fock) defined for each density operator
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
by

(14)

\mathscr{E}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}:=h^{d}\tr\!\left(\left(-\hbar^{2}\Delta+U(x)\right){\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}K(x-y)\,\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}(x)\,\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}(y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\,,

where
U
is a trapping potential and
K
a repulsive singular pair interaction potential. Here, we look at a grand canonical ensemble of fermions. That is, the functional is minimized over all density operators verifying
0\leq{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\leq\mathbf{1}
without fixing the trace, where
\mathbf{1}
denotes the identity operator. We follow the convention that the chemical potential
\mu>0
is included in
U(x)
. The operator bound is a manifestation of the
Pauli Exclusion Principle
: no more than two fermions can occupy the same quantum state.

We are interested in both the regularity properties, as well as the
quantitative
semiclassical limit of the minimizers of the Hartree functional, here and in the sequel denoted by
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}
. Recently, the Weyl law (
2
) and its local version (
6
) for the minimizers
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}
have been studied in
[
48
]
. In this context, the limit of the densities
\varrho_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}}
is determined via Thomas–Fermi theory, and solves the fixed point equation

(15)

\varrho_{\textnormal{TF}}=\omega_{d}\left(U+K*\varrho_{\textnormal{TF}}\right)_{-}^{d/2}.

In particular, it has been proven that the Weyl law and its local version hold

(16)

\lim_{\hbar\to 0^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}}(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{\textnormal{TF}}(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{ and }\quad\lim_{\hbar\to 0^{+}}\varrho_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}}=\varrho_{\textnormal{TF}}

where the last limit holds weakly in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{1+d/2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. In one of our main results, we give a quantitative
L^{p}
-version of the local Weyl law, based on the regularity that is inherited from the commutator estimates satisfied by
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}
.

For
N
-particle systems, establishing the limit (
16
) as
N\rightarrow\infty
of the position density of the ground state
\Psi_{N}
goes back to
[
43
]
, who analyzed Coulomb systems. The problem has been recently revisited in a modern perspective by
[
21
]
, where both the local Weyl law and the phase space Weyl law are established for a wide range of potentials. These convergence results were extended in
[
40
]
to states at positive temperature, in
[
22
]
to systems with magnetic fields, and in
[
26
]
to anyons. At zero temperature, the convergence of states was improved from weak to strong convergence in
[
12
]
. See also the approach via Gamma-convergence in
[
29
]
.

1.4.
Main results

1.4.1.
Commutator estimates and Weyl’s law

We consider Schrödinger operators with
C^{1,\alpha}
potentials which grow at infinity. Our first main result on commutator estimates is the following theorem. Here and in the sequel
\nabla^{2}
denotes the Hessian.

Theorem 1.1
.

For
\hbar\in(0,1)
, let
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\mathds{1}_{H\leq 0}
where
H=-\hbar^{2}\Delta+V
in
d=3
. Assume that for some
\beta\geq 0

(H1)

V\in C^{1,1/2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\,,\qquad\lim_{\left|{x}\right|\to\infty}V(x)=\infty\,,\qquad e^{-\beta\left|{x}\right|}\,\nabla V\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})

uniformly in
\hbar
. Then, for any
p\in[1,\infty)
, there exists a constant
C>0
independent of
\hbar
such that

\displaystyle h^{d}\tr\!\left(\left|{\left[{x,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right]}\right|^{p}\right)\leq C\,\hbar\quad\text{ and }\quad h^{d}\tr\!\left(\left|{\left[{\hbar\nabla,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right]}\right|^{p}\right)\leq\begin{cases}C\,\hbar&\text{ if }p\geq 2\\
C\,\hbar\left|{\ln\hbar}\right|^{2-p}&\text{ if }p\leq 2\,.\end{cases}

Additionally, if
e^{-\beta\left|{x}\right|}\,\nabla^{2}V\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
uniformly in
\hbar
, then the
\left|{\ln\hbar}\right|
factor can be removed. That is, for any
1\leq p<\infty
there exists a constant
C>0
independent of
\hbar
such that

\displaystyle h^{d}\tr\!\left(\left|{\left[{\hbar\nabla,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right]}\right|^{p}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\,\hbar\,.

Remark 1.4.1
.

It follows from the proof of Theorem
1.1
that we can give explicit values of the constants
C>0
in terms of
V
and the constant
\mathcal{C}_{1}>0
from the local eigenvalue estimate (
4
).

Remark 1.4.2
.

All the bounds that contain no
\left|{\ln\hbar}\right|
in our theorem, such as the Hilbert–Schmidt bounds (the case
p=2
) are optimal in terms of
\hbar
. Indeed, this can be checked in the case of the harmonic oscillator
V(x)=\left|{x}\right|^{2}-\mu
(see e.g.
[
6
,
39
]
).

Remark 1.4.3
.

Bounds on commutators with
x
allow more generally to obtain bounds on commutators with operators of multiplication by Lipschitz continuous functions. Indeed, by
[
49
, Inequality (14)]
, for any
p\in(1,\infty)
, there exists
c_{p}>0
such that for any
u\in W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

\left\|{\left[{u(x),{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right]}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{p}}\leq c_{p}\left\|{\nabla u}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\left\|{\left[{x,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right]}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{p}}.

Moreover, if
u
is locally Lipschitz but growing at infinity, then one still obtains a similar bound using the Agmon inequality (see Proposition
2.4
).

In terms of the quantum gradients defined in Equation (
12
), when
e^{-\beta\left|{x}\right|}\,\nabla^{2}V\in L^{\infty}
, the estimates in Theorem
1.1
can be written for any
p\in[1,\infty]

\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{p}}\leq\frac{C}{\hbar^{1/p^{\prime}}}\quad\text{ and }\quad\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{p}}\leq\frac{C}{\hbar^{1/p^{\prime}}}\,,

where
p^{\prime}=\frac{p}{p-1}
, or even more compactly, defining
\boldsymbol{\nabla}{\boldsymbol{\rho}}=(\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}{\boldsymbol{\rho}})
so that
\left|{\boldsymbol{\nabla}{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\right|^{2}=\left|{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\right|^{2}+\left|{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\right|^{2}
,

(17)

\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nabla}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{p}}\leq\frac{C}{\hbar^{1/p^{\prime}}}\,.

The constant
C
is independent of
\hbar
and can be taken independent of
p
. Recall Definition (
5
). Since
\nabla\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}
, the case
p=1
implies the following
W^{1,1}
estimates

Corollary 1.2

(Regularity of the density)
.

Assuming
V
satisfies (
H1
), there exists a constant
C>0
independent of
\hbar
so that

\left\|{\nabla\varrho_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}}\right\|_{L^{1}}\leq C\left|{\ln\hbar}\right|.

Additionally, if
e^{-\beta\left|{x}\right|}\,\nabla^{2}V\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, the logarithm can be removed. That is

\left\|{\nabla\varrho_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}}\right\|_{L^{1}}\leq C\,.

As written in Section
1.2
, the divergence as
\hbar\to 0
of the right-hand side of Inequality (
17
) is coherent with the fact that nonzero characteristic functions are not smooth. As reviewed e.g. in
[
54
]
, the correct spaces to measure the regularity of characteristic functions in
L^{p}
with
p\in(1,\infty)
are the Besov spaces
B^{1/p}_{p,\infty}
, which can be seen as a
L^{p}
generalization of Hölder spaces of order
1/p
. More precisely, if the regularity order
s\in(0,1)
, the spaces
B^{s}_{p,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
are Banach spaces with respect to the norm
\left\|{f}\right\|_{B^{s}_{p,\infty}}=\left\|{f}\right\|_{L^{p}}+\left\|{f}\right\|_{\dot{B}^{s}_{p,\infty}}
, where one defines for
f:\mathbb{R}^{2d}\to\mathbb{C}
the homogeneous seminorm

\left\|{f}\right\|_{\dot{B}^{s}_{p,\infty}}:=\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\left\|{f(\cdot-z)-f}\right\|_{L^{p}}}{\left|{z}\right|^{s}}\,.

They verify for any
\epsilon>0
,
W^{s,p}\subset B^{s}_{p,\infty}\subset W^{s-\epsilon,p}
, where
W^{s,p}
are Sobolev spaces. Analogous semi-norms can be defined in the quantum setting. The phase space shift of an operator being given for
z_{0}=(x_{0},\xi_{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
by

\mathsf{T}_{z_{0}}{\boldsymbol{\rho}}=\tau_{z_{0}}\,{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\,\tau_{-z_{0}}\quad\text{ where }\quad\tau_{z_{0}}\varphi(x)=e^{i\,\xi_{0}\cdot x/\hbar}\,\varphi(x-x_{0})\,,

one can define as in
[
38
]
the quantum homogeneous Besov seminorms

\left\|{{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\right\|_{\dot{\mathcal{B}}^{s}_{p,\infty}}=\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\frac{\left\|{\mathsf{T}_{z}{\boldsymbol{\rho}}-{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{p}}}{\left|{z}\right|^{s}}.

They satisfy in particular for
p=2
,
\left\|{f_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\right\|_{\dot{B}^{s}_{2,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}=\left\|{{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\right\|_{\dot{\mathcal{B}}^{s}_{2,\infty}}
. As stated in the next theorem, one then recovers that these norms are bounded uniformly in
\hbar
, and the situation is even slightly better in the quantum case, where a regularization at scale
\hbar
can be observed.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
\hbar\in(0,1)
and
V
satisfy (
H1
) and assume
e^{-\beta\left|{x}\right|}\,\nabla^{2}V\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. Then, there exists a constant
C
independent of
\hbar
such that for all
p\in[1,\infty]
and
z\in\mathbb{R}^{6}
,

\left\|{\mathsf{T}_{z}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{p}}\leq C\min\!\left(\frac{\left|{z}\right|}{\hbar^{1/p^{\prime}}},\left|{z}\right|^{1/p},1\right).

In particular,
\left\|{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\dot{\mathcal{B}}^{1/p}_{p,\infty}}
is bounded uniformly in
\hbar
for any
p\in[1,\infty]
, and for
p=2
, one obtains that
f_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in B^{1/2}_{2,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{6})
and so in
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{6})
with
s<1/2
uniformly in
\hbar
. As in the previous theorems, if
V
is only in
C^{1,1/2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, then the estimates remain true up to the multiplication by
\left|{\ln\hbar}\right|^{2/p-1}
when
p\leq 2
.

Remark 1.4.4
.

Taking
z=(0,\hbar\,\xi)
in the above theorem and using the fact that
\tau_{z}
is unitary gives the following commutator estimate. For any
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,

h^{d}\tr\!\left(\left|{\left[{e^{i\,\xi\cdot x},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right]}\right|^{p}\right)\leq C\min(\left|{\xi}\right|^{p-1}\!,1)\left|{\xi}\right|\hbar\,.

Now, let us state our results concerning the quantitative convergence of the densities in the semiclassical limit. Recall the definitions of the position densities
\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
in (
5
) and
\varrho_{f}
in (
10
), the Wigner function
f_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
in (
7
) and the Weyl quantization
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{f}
in (
8
). We have the following theorem.

Theorem 1.4

(Linear quantitative convergence)
.

Let
\hbar\in(0,1)
,
d=3
and
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\mathds{1}_{H\leq 0}
with
H=-\hbar^{2}\Delta+V
and
f=\mathds{1}_{\left|{\xi}\right|^{2}+V\leq 0}
. Assuming
V
satisfies (
H1
), there is a constant
C>0
independent of
\hbar
such that

\displaystyle\left\|{\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-\varrho_{f}}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\,\Big(1+\left\|{\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\dot{B}^{1/2}_{2,\infty}}^{2/3}\Big)\,\hbar^{1/3}

\displaystyle\left\|{\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-\varrho_{f}}\right\|_{L^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\left\|{\nabla\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{L^{1}}^{1/2}\right)\hbar^{1/2}

\displaystyle\left\|{f_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-f}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\left\|{\nabla\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{L^{1}}^{1/2}\right)\hbar^{1/4}

\displaystyle\lVert{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{f}\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nabla}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{1}}\right)\hbar^{1/2}\,.

Additionally, if
V\in W^{2,1}(\Omega)
for an open set
\Omega
containing
\{V<0\}
, then there exists
C>0
such that

\left\|{f_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-f}\right\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\,\hbar^{1/4}.

Here,
\mathcal{L}^{1}
denotes the scaled trace norm defined in Equation (
13
) and the values of the constants
C
can be made explicit (see Proposition
4.7
).

Remark 1.4.5
.

The gradients
\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nabla}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{1}}
and
\left\|{\nabla\varrho_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}}\right\|_{L^{1}}
grow at most logarithmically in
\hbar
thanks to Theorem
1.1
. Note also that the Besov norm
\dot{B}_{2,\infty}^{1/2}
can be controlled via interpolation with
\left\|{\varrho_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}
and
\left\|{\nabla\varrho_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}}\right\|_{L^{1}}
, see e.g. Remark
1.4.11
. In particular, if
e^{-\beta\left|{x}\right|}\,\nabla^{2}V\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
uniformly in
\hbar
, then under the hypotheses of Theorem
1.4
, one obtains the existence of a constant
C
independent of
\hbar
such that

\left\|{\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-\varrho_{f}}\right\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\,\hbar^{1/3}\qquad\left\|{\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-\varrho_{f}}\right\|_{L^{1}}\leq C\,\hbar^{1/2}

and

\left\|{f_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-f}\right\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\,\hbar^{1/4}\qquad\lVert{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{f}\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{1}}\leq C\,\hbar^{1/2}\,.

Remark 1.4.6
.

In general, it is not expected that the trace-class norm of an operator
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
controls the
L^{1}
-norm of its Wigner transform
f_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
. However, denoting
\widehat{f}
the Fourier transform of a function
f:\mathbb{R}^{2d}\to\mathbb{C}
, we have Groenewold’s formula
[
30
]

\widehat{f}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}(\xi,\eta)=h^{d}\tr\!\left(e^{-2i\pi\left(x\cdot\xi+\eta\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\right)}{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right)

with
\boldsymbol{p}=-i\hbar\nabla
. It implies the bound in Fourier space

(18)

\left\|{\widehat{f}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-\widehat{f}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nabla}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{1}}\right)\hbar^{1/2}\,.

Remark 1.4.7
.

Our bounds imply convergence in Wasserstein distance. Indeed, if
W_{p}(\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}},\varrho_{f})
denotes the Wasserstein distance of order
p
between
\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
and
\varrho_{f}
, then for any
p\in[1,\infty)
it holds (see e.g.
[
56
, Proposition 7.10]
)

(19)

W_{p}(\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}},\varrho_{f})\leq 2\left\|{\left|{x}\right|^{p}\left(\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-\varrho_{f}\right)}\right\|_{L^{1}}^{1/p}.

Taking
R>0
sufficiently large so that the ball
B_{R}
of radius
R
contains the support of
\varrho_{f}
and such that
\left\|{\left|{x}\right|^{p}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{L^{1}(B_{R}^{c})}\leq\sqrt{\hbar}
by Agmon’s estimates (Proposition
2.4
), it follows that the right-hand side of Equation (
19
) is bounded by
2R\left\|{\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-\varrho_{f}}\right\|_{L^{1}(B_{R})}^{1/p}+2\,\hbar^{\frac{1}{2p}}
which gives

W_{p}(\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}},\varrho_{f})\leq C\,\hbar^{\frac{1}{2p}}.

1.4.2.
The interacting case

In the Hartree case, we consider the minimizers of the functional
\mathscr{E}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
with an external trap
U(x)
and a singular pair potential
K(x-y)
which we assume to be repulsive. Our results can cover Coulomb singularities, at the expense of adding a logarithmic factor
\sqrt{\left|{\ln\hbar}\right|}
in the estimates. Currently, we do not know if this factor is optimal and a feature of the theory, or a consequence of the proof. For potentials that are less singular than Coulomb, we obtain the desired optimal commutator estimates.

Our main results covering commutator estimates for interacting systems reads as follows.

Theorem 1.5

(Non-linear commutator estimates)
.

For
\hbar\in(0,1)
and in
d=3
, let
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}
be a minimizer of the Hartree energy
\mathscr{E}
given by (
14
). Assume that the interaction potential
K:\mathbb{R}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is of the form

(H2)

K(x)=\kappa\left|{x}\right|^{-a}\quad\text{ with }\quad\kappa\geq 0

where
a\in[0,1]
, and that the external potential
U:\mathbb{R}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfies, for some
\beta\geq 0

(H3)

\lim_{\left|{x}\right|\to\infty}U(x)=\infty\,,\qquad e^{-\beta\left|{x}\right|}\,\nabla^{2}U\,\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\,.

Let
p\in[1,\infty)
. Then, if
a\in(0,1)
there exists
C>0
independent of
\hbar
such that

h^{d}\tr\!\left(\left|{\left[{x,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}}\right]}\right|^{p}\right)\leq C\,\hbar\quad\text{ and }\quad h^{d}\tr\!\left(\left|{\left[{\hbar\nabla,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}}\right]}\right|^{p}\right)\leq C\,\hbar

while if
a=1
there exists
C>0
independent of
\hbar
such that

\displaystyle h^{d}\tr\!\left(\left|{\left[{x,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}}\right]}\right|^{p}\right)\leq C\,\hbar\quad\text{ and }\quad h^{d}\tr\!\left(\left|{\left[{\hbar\nabla,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}}\right]}\right|^{p}\right)\leq\begin{cases}C\,\hbar&\text{ if }p\geq 2\\
C\,\hbar\left|{\ln\hbar}\right|^{\frac{2-p}{2}}&\text{ if }p\leq 2\,.\end{cases}

The values of the constants
C
can be made explicit in terms of
V
and the analogous constant
\mathcal{C}_{1}>0
.

Remark 1.4.8
.

In the Coulomb case
a=1
, this gives for
p=1
the commutator estimates

\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{1}}\leq C\quad\text{ and }\quad\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{1}}\leq C\left|{\ln\hbar}\right|^{1/2}.

The fact that we obtain a
\left|{\ln\hbar}\right|^{1/2}
rather than a
\left|{\ln\hbar}\right|
correction is useful for applications.
Indeed, in works such as
[
10
,
8
]
one typically obtain errors of the form

\left\|{{\boldsymbol{\rho}}(t)-{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{2}(t)}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{p}}\leq\frac{C}{N^{\alpha}}\,e^{C_{t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nabla}{\boldsymbol{\rho}}(0)}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{1}}}

for some constant
\alpha>0
, with
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{2}
solutions of two different evolution equations and
N=\hbar^{-d}
, and
C_{t}
some constant growing with time. If
\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nabla}{\boldsymbol{\rho}}(0)}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{1}}
was of size
\left|{\ln\hbar}\right|
, then we get an error
\frac{C}{N^{\alpha}}\,\hbar^{-C_{t}}=C\,\hbar^{\alpha d-C_{t}}
which would become large in finite time. On the other hand, if
\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nabla}{\boldsymbol{\rho}}(0)}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{1}}
is of size
\left|{\ln\hbar}\right|^{1/2}
, the error
remains small globally in time. This is because the error is of the form
\frac{C}{N^{\alpha}}\,e^{C_{t}\sqrt{\left|{\ln\hbar}\right|}}=C\,e^{C_{t}\sqrt{\left|{\ln\hbar}\right|}-\alpha d\left|{\ln\hbar}\right|}
.

Remark 1.4.9
.

Similarly as in the linear theory, we obtain the estimates for the densities

(20)

\left\|{\nabla\varrho_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}}}\right\|_{L^{1}}\leq C\,,\quad\text{ if }a<1\quad\text{ and }\quad\left\|{\nabla\varrho_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}}}\right\|_{L^{1}}\leq C\left|{\ln\hbar}\right|^{1/2},\quad\text{ if }a=1\,.

Finally, we turn to our last main result, regarding the quantitative convergence of position densities, and of states. Recalling that
\varrho_{\textnormal{TF}}(x)
is the associated minimizer of the Thomas–Fermi functional, solving the fixed point equation (
15
), we let the associated classical phase space distribution be

(21)

f_{\textnormal{TF}}(x,\xi):=\mathds{1}(\left|{\xi}\right|\leq\omega_{d}^{-1/d}\varrho_{\textnormal{TF}}^{{1/d}})\,,

for
(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then, we obtain the following quantitative result. Here again,
\mathcal{L}^{1}
denotes the scaled trace norm defined in (
13
).

Theorem 1.6

(Non-linear quantitative convergence)
.

For
\hbar\in(0,1)
and
d=3
, let
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}
be a minimizer of the Hartree energy
\mathscr{E}
given by Equation (
14
).
Let
\varrho_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}}
be its density, and
f_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}}
its Wigner transform.
Denote by
\varrho_{\textnormal{TF}}
and
f_{\textnormal{TF}}
the respective limits, given by (
15
) and (
21
). Assuming the potentials verify hypotheses (
H2
) with
a\in(0,1]
and (
H3
), there is a constant
C>0
independent of
\hbar
such that

\displaystyle\left\|{f_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}}-f_{\textnormal{TF}}}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\,\hbar^{1/4}

\displaystyle\left\|{\varrho_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}}-\varrho_{\textnormal{TF}}}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\,\big(1+\left\|{\varrho_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}}}\right\|_{\dot{B}^{1/2}_{2,\infty}}^{2/3}\big)\,\hbar^{1/3}\,.

Moreover, in the case when
0<a<1
, it holds

\left\|{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}-{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{f_{\textnormal{TF}}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{1}}\leq C\,\hbar^{1/2}\quad\text{ and }\quad\left\|{\varrho_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}}-\varrho_{\textnormal{TF}}}\right\|_{L^{1}}\leq C\,\hbar^{1/2}

while in the Coulomb case
a=1
, one obtains

\left\|{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}-{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{f_{\textnormal{TF}}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{1}}\leq C\,\hbar^{1/2}\left|{\ln\hbar}\right|^{1/2}\quad\text{ and }\quad\left\|{\varrho_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}}-\varrho_{\textnormal{TF}}}\right\|_{L^{1}}\leq C\,\hbar^{1/2}\left|{\ln\hbar}\right|^{1/4}.

Additionally, if
\hbar^{4}\,e^{-\beta\left|{x}\right|}\,\nabla^{5}U\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
uniformly in
\hbar
, then there exists a constant
C>0
independent of
\hbar
such that for any
0<a\leq 1

\left\|{\varrho_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}}-\varrho_{\textnormal{TF}}}\right\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\,\hbar^{1/3}\,.

Remark 1.4.10
.

A similar proof also yields estimates in
L^{q}
for
\varrho_{\textnormal{H}}
for any
q\in[1,2]
, with rate
\hbar^{\frac{1}{q+1}}
, and an additional logarithmic factor if
a=1
. Of course, since
\left\|{\varrho_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}
is bounded uniformly in
\hbar
in our analysis, one also gets convergence for
q\geq 2
by interpolation (i.e. Hölder inequality), but in this case with a rate
\hbar^{1/(3q)}
. Similarly, one obtains estimates in
\mathcal{L}^{q}
for
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}
. Notice however that these do not imply convergence of the Wigner transform in
L^{q}
with
q<2
. Such results could still be obtained for example using weighted
L^{2}
estimates as in
[
39
]
.

Remark 1.4.11
.

In our analysis,
\left\|{\varrho_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}
is bounded uniformly in
\hbar
. Thus, thanks to Hölder’s inequality, we obtain that under hypotheses (
H2
) and (
H3
)

\left\|{\varrho_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}}}\right\|_{\dot{B}^{1/2}_{2,\infty}}\leq C\left\|{\nabla\varrho_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}}}\right\|_{L^{1}}^{1/2}.

Note that the right-hand side introduces a logarithmic factor
\left|{\ln\hbar}\right|
for the Coulomb case
a=1
. On the other hand, under the additional condition
\hbar^{4}\,e^{-\beta\left|{x}\right|}\,\nabla^{5}U\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
uniformly in
\hbar
, we prove that the following are bounded uniformly in
\hbar

(22)

\left\|{\varrho_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}}}\right\|_{\dot{B}^{1/2}_{2,\infty}},\quad\sqrt{\hbar}\left\|{\nabla\varrho_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}}}\right\|_{L^{2}},\quad\text{ and }\quad\left\|{\varrho_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}}}\right\|_{H^{s}},\quad\text{ for }s<1/2\,.

See Proposition
5.2
. The reader should not confuse the estimates (
22
) with those for
f_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}}
. The latter follow essentially from Theorem
1.5
, whereas the validity of those of
\varrho_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}}
must be established via
weighted
commutator estimates.

1.5.
Strategy of proof

Let us summarize here some of the main ideas involved in our proofs. First, for the linear commutator estimates in Theorem
1.1
, one of the main ingredients is the following local eigenvalue estimate

(23)

h^{d}\tr\mathds{1}_{[a,b]}(H)\leq\mathcal{C}_{1}\left(\left|{b-a}\right|+\hbar\right)

where
\mathcal{C}_{1}>0
is a distinguished constant (depending only on
V
) and
a<b
are appropriate real numbers sufficiently close to
0
. In our analysis, we derive (
23
) from Weyl’s law for
C^{1,\alpha}
potentials. Equipped now with local eigenvalue estimate (
23
), we can prove the following singular resolvent estimate

h^{d}\tr\!\left(\mathds{1}_{H\leq 0}\left(\hbar-H\right)^{-2}\right)\leq\mathcal{C}_{2}\,\hbar

see e.g. Section
3.1
for its motivation and a simple example. Its role in the proof of the commutator estimates is crucial and is developed in Section
3
. Let us mention that the connection between (
23
) and commutator estimates was first established in
[
23
]
. Here, while we start from the same local eigenvalue estimate, we do not employ additional pseudo-differential operators methods. Instead, our methods are based on the understanding of correlations between eigenfunctions of
H
, created by
x
or
\boldsymbol{p}
. In this regard, our estimates borrow inspiration from
[
6
]
.

Our technique seems to be also related to the double operator integral techniques which were introduced in
[
17
]
, studied more systematically by Birman and Solomyak
[
11
]
and allowed to obtain the characterization of operator-lipschitz functions in Schatten spaces
[
49
]
, which is a problem closely related to commutator estimates. We refer the reader to the survey
[
3
]
for details and additional references in the huge literature on the subject.

As for the proof of the linear convergence in Theorem
1.4
, it is based on two steps. First, we use the well-known methods of coherent states to show via variational methods that the Husimi measure of
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\mathds{1}_{H\leq 0}
is close to the limit
f=\mathds{1}_{\left|{\xi}\right|^{2}+V\leq 0}
. Secondly, we compare the Wigner function to the Husimi measure via convolution estimates — here, the regularity of
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
and
\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
enter the estimate in Theorem
1.4
.

As for the interacting setting, one of the main ingredients is a representation formula for the minimizers
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}
of
\mathscr{E}
. Namely, let us denote the effective one-body Hamiltonian by

H_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}:=-\hbar^{2}\Delta+V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}(x)\quad\text{ with }\quad V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}:=K*\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}+U\,.

Then, the minimizers satisfy the fixed-point equation

{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}=\mathds{1}_{(-\infty,0)}(H_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}})+{\boldsymbol{q}}\quad\text{ where }\quad\ran{\boldsymbol{q}}\subset\ker H_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}}

for some self-adjoint
0\leq{\boldsymbol{q}}\leq\mathbf{1}
, see e.g.
[
48
]
. This fixed point equation will then establish the connection to the linear theory, after one proves several
apriori estimates
for the (
\hbar
-dependent) non-linear interaction potential

V_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}}=U+K*\varrho_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}}\,.

In particular, we prove that its
C^{1,1/2}_{\mathrm{loc}}
semi-norm is uniformly bounded in
\hbar
, which yields the optimal Weyl laws for
H_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
and as a corollary the local eigenvalue estimate (
23
).

Finally, let us discuss the differences occurring in the choice of
a
for
K(x)=\kappa\left|{x}\right|^{-a}
. The case
a\in[0,1)
is
sub-critical
and we obtain the optimal commutator estimates (
11
) for the minimizers of
\mathscr{E}
. The case
a=1
corresponds to the Coulomb potential and becomes the
critical case
, where we can only show the commutator estimates (
11
) with an additional logarithmic factor
\left|{\ln\hbar}\right|
. The key difference between these two regimes comes from the estimates that are satisfied by the mean-field potential

(24)

\varrho\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\implies K*\varrho\in C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\,.

Let us further explain. First, we observe that the CLR bound and standard Agmon estimates imply
\lVert\varrho_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}}\rVert_{L^{1}}\leq C
and
\lVert\varrho_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq C
, respectively. In our proof, in order to obtain the optimal commutator estimates in the trace-class norm, we require boundedness of the
C^{2}
norm of the mean-field potential
K*\varrho_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textnormal{H}}}
, uniformly in
\hbar
. In particular, since the map
\varrho\mapsto K*\varrho
is continuous for
0<a<1
from
L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}\to C^{2}_{b}
, this is easily obtained via the
L^{1}
and
L^{\infty}
estimates. On the other hand, for the Coulomb case, we lose the continuity of the map, and are unable to make this strategy work.

2.
A priori estimates

Throughout this section, we denote, in dimension
d\geq 1

{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\mathds{1}_{H\leq 0}\quad\text{ with }\quad H=-\hbar^{2}\Delta+V

for some real-valued
V\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
V_{-}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. We state several estimates on
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
, quantitatively with respect to the potential
V
. Let us introduce here the notation for the momentum operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, to be used in the rest of the paper

\boldsymbol{p}:=-i\hbar\nabla\,.

2.1.
L^{p}
estimates

Recall that the position density
\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
associated to
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
was defined in (
5
). Let us first notice that the
L^{1}
norm of
\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
is bounded uniformly in
\hbar
by the Cwikel–Lieb–Rozenblum inequality
[
52
,
16
,
41
]
in dimension
d\geq 3
. That is, there exists a constant
\mathsf{L}_{0,d}>0
such that

(25)

\left\|{\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{L^{1}}\leq\mathsf{L}_{0,d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}V_{-}^{d/2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,.

In lower dimensions, one can use the fact that
V_{-}
is compactly supported and bounded and
V
is growing at infinity as in
[
50
, Theorem XIII.81]
.

Combined with the
L^{1}
bound (
25
) and Agmon’s estimates (established below), the following lemma implies
uniform bounds for all
L^{p}
norms of
\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
1\leq d\leq 3
. Then, there exists
C_{d}>0
such that

\left\|{\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C_{d}\left(1+\left\|{V_{-}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}+\left\|{V{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\right).

Proof.

Let
u\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be a non-negative test function. Then, we have for all
s>0

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u\,\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=h^{d}\tr\!\left(u\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)=\left\|{\sqrt{u}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq\left\|{\sqrt{u}\left\langle\boldsymbol{p}\right\rangle^{-s}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}^{2}\left\|{\left\langle\boldsymbol{p}\right\rangle^{s}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}^{2}

where
\left\langle\boldsymbol{p}\right\rangle^{2}=\mathbf{1}+\boldsymbol{p}^{*}\boldsymbol{p}
. For
s>\frac{d}{2}
we compute
\lVert\sqrt{u}\left\langle\boldsymbol{p}\right\rangle^{-s}\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}^{2}=\left\|{u}\right\|_{L^{1}}\lVert\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{-2s}\rVert_{L^{1}_{\xi}}=C(s,d)\left\|{u}\right\|_{L^{1}}
. We therefore find for
d\leq 3
that with
s=2

(26)

\left\|{\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C_{d}\left\|{\left\langle\boldsymbol{p}\right\rangle^{2}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\leq C_{d}\left(1+\lVert\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{2}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\right).

The last term can be estimated as follows

(27)

\left\|{\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{2}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\leq\left\|{H{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}+\left\|{V{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\,.

It suffices now to use
H\geq-\left\|{V_{-}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}
. This finishes the proof.
∎

2.2.
Energy estimates

Another common way to obtain bounds on the
L^{p}
norms with
p<\infty
is through Lieb–Thirring inequalities
[
44
]
, which give for instance the following quantum analogue of a classical interpolation inequality used in kinetic theory (see e.g.
[
45
]
)

(28)

\left\|{\varrho_{{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}}\right\|_{L^{p}}\leq\left(C_{\textnormal{LT}}\left\|{{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\right)^{1/p^{\prime}}\left(h^{d}\tr\!\left(\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{2}\!{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right)\right)^{1/p}

for
d\geq 1
. Here,
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
is a density operator,
p=1+\frac{2}{d}
and
C_{\textnormal{LT}}
only depends on
d
. It gives in particular the following natural upper bounds for the kinetic and potential energy of the spectral function
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
in terms of
V_{-}
.

Lemma 2.2
.

There holds

h^{d}\tr\!\left(\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{2}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}V_{-}\leq C_{\textnormal{LT}}\left\|{V_{-}}\right\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}}^{p^{\prime}}

where
p^{\prime}=1+\frac{d}{2}
.

Proof.

The first inequality just follows from the fact that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}V_{-}-h^{d}\tr\!\left(\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{2}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)=h^{d}\tr\!\left(H{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}V_{+}\leq 0
. Combining this inequality with Hölder’s inequality, the fact that
0\leq{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\leq\mathbf{1}
and the Lieb–Thirring inequality (
28
) gives

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}V_{-}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\rho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{L^{p}}\left\|{V_{-}}\right\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}}\leq C_{\textnormal{LT}}^{1/p^{\prime}}\left(h^{d}\tr\!\left(\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{2}\!{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right)\right)^{1/p}\left\|{V_{-}}\right\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\textnormal{LT}}^{1/p^{\prime}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\,V_{-}\right)^{1/p}\left\|{V_{-}}\right\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}}

from which the second inequality follows.
∎

2.3.
Agmon-like estimates

In order to establish some decay properties of the density
\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
at infinity, we review here the well-known Agmon estimates
[
1
,
2
]
for the density operator
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
.

Lemma 2.3

(Energy estimate)
.

Let
d\geq 1
. Then, for any smooth function
u:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
,

(29)

\tr\!\left((V-\hbar^{2}\left|{\nabla u}\right|^{2})\,e^{2u}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)=\tr\!\left(e^{2u}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,H\right)-\tr\!\left(\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{2}e^{u}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,e^{u}\right)\leq 0\,.

Proof.

By the Leibniz rule for commutators and the fact that
\left[{\boldsymbol{p},e^{u}}\right]=-i\hbar\,e^{u}\,\nabla u

\displaystyle\left[{H,e^{u}}\right]=\left[{\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{2},e^{u}}\right]

\displaystyle=-i\hbar\left(\boldsymbol{p}\cdot e^{u}\nabla u+e^{u}\nabla u\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\right)

\displaystyle=-i\hbar\,e^{u}\left(\boldsymbol{p}\cdot\nabla u+\nabla u\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\right)-\hbar^{2}\left|{\nabla u}\right|^{2}e^{u}.

Therefore, by cyclicity of the trace

\tr\!\left(H\,e^{2u}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)=\tr\!\left(\left[{H,e^{u}}\right]e^{u}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)+\tr\!\left(H\,e^{u}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,e^{u}\right)\\
=-i\hbar\tr\!\left(e^{u}\left(\boldsymbol{p}\cdot\nabla u+\nabla u\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\right)e^{u}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)-\hbar^{2}\tr\!\left(\left|{\nabla u}\right|^{2}e^{2u}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)+\tr\!\left(H\,e^{u}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,e^{u}\right).

Since
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,H
is self-adjoint,
\tr(H\,e^{2u}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})=\tr({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,H\,e^{2u})\in\mathbb{R}
. Moreover,
\tr(\left|{\nabla u}\right|^{2}e^{2u}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})
and
\tr(H\,e^{u}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,e^{u})
are also real because
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
is self-adjoint. On the other side, the term
i\hbar\tr\!\left(e^{u}\left(\boldsymbol{p}\cdot\nabla u+\nabla u\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\right)e^{u}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)
is imaginary. Hence, taking the real part of the previous equation yields

\tr\!\left(H\,e^{2u}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)=-\hbar^{2}\tr\!\left(\left|{\nabla u}\right|^{2}e^{2u}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)+\tr\!\left(H\,e^{u}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,e^{u}\right)

which can also be written

\tr\!\left((V-\hbar^{2}\left|{\nabla u}\right|^{2})\,e^{2u}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)=\tr\!\left(e^{2u}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,H\right)-\tr\!\left(\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{2}e^{u}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,e^{u}\right).

Since
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,H\leq 0
, it implies the result.
∎

The above lemma implies both exponential decay in
x
and smallness in
\hbar
in the region where the potential is positive. In the following proposition, we write operator versions of Agmon’s estimates on exponential decay away from the classically allowed regions. For
0\leq a<b
we use the notation

(30)

\Omega_{a}:=\{V\leq a\}\,,\quad\Omega_{a,b}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\dist\!\left(x,\Omega_{a}\right)<b\}=\Omega_{a}+B_{b}

where
B_{r}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\left|{x}\right|<r\}
. We also use the distance function

(31)

d_{a}(x):=\dist\!\left(x,\Omega_{a}\right).

Proposition 2.4

(Smallness outside of the bulk)
.

Let
d\geq 1
and
a>0
. Then, the following statements are true. For any
0<\alpha<a

(32)

h^{d}\tr\!\left(\mathds{1}_{V>a}\,e^{2\sqrt{\alpha}\,d_{a}/\hbar}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)\leq\frac{1}{a-\alpha}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,V_{-}\,.

For any
R>0
,

(33)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{1,R}^{c}}e^{\frac{d_{1}}{2\hbar}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{3}\,e^{-\frac{R}{2\hbar}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,V_{-}\,,

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{1,R}^{c}}e^{\frac{d_{1}}{4\hbar}}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{d}}{R^{d/2}}\,e^{-\frac{R}{8\hbar}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,V_{-}\right)^{1/2}

where
C_{d}=2/\sqrt{3}\left(2d/(\pi e)\right)^{d/2}
. For all
\beta>0
such that
\beta\,\hbar\leq 1/4
,

(34)

\left\|{e^{\beta\,d_{1}}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\leq e^{\beta}+C_{d}\,e^{-\,\frac{1}{8\hbar}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,V_{-}\right)^{1/2}.

In particular, let
r\geq 1
be such that
\Omega_{1,1}\subset B_{r}
. Then, for
0\leq\beta\leq 1/4\hbar

(35)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{\beta\left|{x}\right|}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\leq C\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\left(1+V_{-}\right),

with
C=e^{2\beta r}+4/(3\sqrt{e})
.

Proof of Proposition
2.4
.

Take
u=\sqrt{\alpha}\,d_{a}/\hbar
in Inequality (
29
). Then
\left|{\nabla u}\right|=\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}}{\hbar}\,\mathds{1}_{V>a}
and Inequality (
29
) gives

h^{d}\tr\!\left((V-\alpha\mathds{1}_{V>a})\,e^{2u}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)\leq 0\,.

This implies that

h^{d}\tr\!\left(\left(V-\alpha\right)\mathds{1}_{V>a}\,e^{2u}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)\leq-h^{d}\tr\!\left(V\,\mathds{1}_{V\leq a}\,e^{2u}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,V_{-}\,,

which proves Inequality (
32
). Let us now take
a=1
and
\alpha=1/4
to find that

(36)

h^{d}\tr\!\left(\mathds{1}_{V>1}\,e^{\,d_{1}/\hbar}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)\leq\frac{4}{3}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,V_{-}\,.

Inequality (
33
) now follows by noticing that
x\in\Omega_{1,R}^{c}\implies d_{1}(x)\geq(d_{1}(x)+R)/2
. To get the next inequality, observe that

\left\|{\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{1,R}^{c}}e^{\frac{d_{1}}{4\hbar}}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}^{2}=\left\|{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{1,R}^{c}}e^{\frac{d_{1}}{2\hbar}}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\leq\tr\!\left(\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{1,R}^{c}}e^{\frac{d_{1}}{2\hbar}}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)=\frac{1}{h^{d}}\int_{\Omega_{1,R}^{c}}e^{\frac{d_{1}}{2\hbar}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,.

Hence it follows from Inequality (
33
) and the fact that for any
t>0
,
t^{d}\,e^{-R\pi t/2}\leq\left(2d/(R\pi e)\right)^{d}

\left\|{\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{1,R}^{c}}e^{\frac{d_{1}}{4\hbar}}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\leq\frac{C_{d}}{R^{d/2}}\,e^{-\frac{R}{8\hbar}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}V_{-}\right)^{1/2}.

Finally, we use the triangle inequality and use
0\leq{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\leq 1
and
\beta\,\hbar\leq 1/4
to get

\left\|{e^{\beta d_{1}}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\leq e^{\beta R}+\left\|{\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{1,R}^{c}}e^{\frac{d_{1}}{4\hbar}}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}},

which implies Inequality (
34
) by taking
R=1
.

For the last inequality, we split the integration region in
B_{2\ell}
and
B_{2\ell}^{c}
. In the first one,
e^{\beta\left|{x}\right|}\leq e^{2\beta\ell}
. In the second one, we use
\beta\left|{x}\right|\leq\frac{\hbar\left|{x}\right|}{4}
and
d_{1}(x)\geq\left|{x}\right|-1\geq\frac{\left|{x}\right|}{2}
, and then (
33
).
∎

We will need to consider some higher order Agmon-like estimates, which can be thought of as Agmon’s estimates for gradients of eigenfunctions, or from a semiclassical point of view, as decay in phase space in both
x
and
\xi
. Recalling that
\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}=h^{d}{\boldsymbol{\rho}}(x,x)
, we look first at
\varrho_{\boldsymbol{p}\cdot{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\,\boldsymbol{p}}
, which is a quantum analogue of
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f\left|{\xi}\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi
. In the case when
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
is diagonalized in the form
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}=\sum_{j\geq 0}\lambda_{j}\ket{\psi_{j}}\bra{\psi_{j}}
for some orthonormal family of eigenvectors
(\psi_{j})_{j\geq 0}
, then

(37)

\varrho_{\boldsymbol{p}\cdot{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\,\boldsymbol{p}}=h^{d}\sum_{j\geq 0}\lambda_{j}\left|{\hbar\nabla\psi_{j}}\right|^{2}.

One can also notice that by the definition of
\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
in the weak sense, for any nonnegative function
\varphi\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{p}\cdot{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\,\boldsymbol{p}}\,\varphi=h^{d}\tr\!\left(\boldsymbol{p}\cdot{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\,\boldsymbol{p}\,\varphi\right)=\left\|{\sqrt{\varphi}\,\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}^{2}.

We will also need similar estimates for higher order gradients. When multiplying two vectors, we will mean their tensor product. In particular, we denote by
\boldsymbol{p}^{k}:=\boldsymbol{p}\otimes\boldsymbol{p}\dots\otimes\boldsymbol{p}
the operator-valued tensor
(\boldsymbol{p}_{j_{1}}\dots\boldsymbol{p}_{j_{k}})_{1\leq j_{1}\dots,j_{k}\leq d}
, and, for tensors of order
2
, denoting their double contraction by
A:B:=\sum_{k}A_{jk}B_{kj}
,

(38)

\varrho_{\boldsymbol{p}^{2}:{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\,\boldsymbol{p}^{2}}=h^{d}\sum_{j\geq 0}\lambda_{j}\left|{\hbar^{2}\nabla^{2}\psi_{j}}\right|^{2}.

with
\nabla^{2}\psi_{j}
denoting the Hessian of
\psi_{j}
. Then, for any nonnegative
\varphi\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{p}^{2}:{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\,\boldsymbol{p}^{2}}\,\varphi=h^{d}\tr\!\left(\boldsymbol{p}^{2}:{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\,\boldsymbol{p}^{2}\,\varphi\right)=\left\|{\sqrt{\varphi}\,\boldsymbol{p}^{2}{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}^{2}.

In the classical case, if
f=\mathds{1}_{\left|{\xi}\right|^{2}\leq V_{-}}
, then we have the following straightforward inequalities

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f\left|{\xi}\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi\leq V_{-}\,\varrho_{f}\quad\text{ and }\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f\left|{\xi}\right|^{4}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi\leq V_{-}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f\left|{\xi}\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi\,.

The following lemmas can be seen as quantum analogues of the above formulas. Recall
d_{1}
is given by (
31
).

Lemma 2.5

(Agmon-like estimate for gradients)
.

Let
d\geq 1
. Then, in the weak sense, there holds

(39)

0\leq\varrho_{\boldsymbol{p}\cdot{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\boldsymbol{p}}\leq\left(\tfrac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\Delta-V\right)\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,.

This and Agmon estimates give that for any
R>0
,

(40)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{1,R}^{c}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{p}\cdot{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\boldsymbol{p}}\,e^{\frac{d_{1}}{2\hbar}}

\displaystyle\leq\,e^{1-\frac{R}{2\hbar}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,V_{-}

(41)

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{1,R}^{c}}\,e^{\frac{d_{1}}{4\hbar}}\,\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{d}}{R^{d/2}}\,e^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{R}{8\hbar}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,V_{-}\right)^{1/2}

with
C_{d}=\left(2d/(\pi e)\right)^{d/2}
. Moreover, for any
\beta>0
such that
\beta\hbar\leq 1/4
,

(42)

\left\|{e^{\beta d_{1}}\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\leq\sqrt{2}\,e^{\beta}\left\|{V_{-}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}^{1/2}+C_{d}\,e^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{8\hbar}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,V_{-}\right)^{1/2}.

Proof.

Let
\varphi\in C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be such that
\varphi\geq 0
. Then since
\left[{\boldsymbol{p},\varphi}\right]=-i\hbar\nabla\varphi
and by cyclicity of the trace

\displaystyle 0\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{p}\cdot{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\boldsymbol{p}}\,\varphi

\displaystyle=h^{d}\tr\!\left(\boldsymbol{p}\cdot{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\boldsymbol{p}\,\varphi\right)=-i\hbar\,h^{d}\tr\!\left(\boldsymbol{p}\cdot{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\nabla\varphi\right)+h^{d}\tr\!\left(\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{2}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\varphi\right)

\displaystyle=-i\hbar\,h^{d}\tr\!\left(\nabla\varphi\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)+h^{d}\tr\!\left(\varphi\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,H\right)-h^{d}\tr\!\left(V\varphi\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)

where
\nabla\varphi
denote the operator of multiplication by
\nabla\varphi
. Taking the real part and observing that
i\hbar\,\boldsymbol{p}\cdot\nabla\varphi-i\hbar\,\nabla\varphi\cdot\boldsymbol{p}=\hbar^{2}\Delta\varphi
yields

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{p}\cdot{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\boldsymbol{p}}\,\varphi=h^{d}\tr\!\left(\tfrac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\,\Delta\varphi\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)+h^{d}\tr\!\left(\varphi\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,H\right)-h^{d}\tr\!\left(V\varphi\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)

and so since
\varphi\geq 0
and
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,H\leq 0
, it follows that

0\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{p}\cdot{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\boldsymbol{p}}\,\varphi\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\left(\tfrac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\Delta\varphi-V\,\varphi\right)

which is the meaning of Inequality (
39
). The previous Agmon estimates together with a standard approximation argument show that we can take
\varphi
to grow exponentially fast at infinity. In particular, taking
\varphi=e^{\frac{v}{\hbar}}
gives

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{p}\cdot{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\boldsymbol{p}}\,e^{\frac{v}{\hbar}}\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\left(\left|{\nabla v}\right|^{2}+\hbar\,\Delta v-2\,V\right)e^{\frac{v}{\hbar}}.

Let us take for instance
v=\left(\hbar^{2}+d_{1}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\geq\hbar
, so that
d_{1}\leq v\leq d_{1}+\hbar
. Then
\left|{\nabla v}\right|=\frac{d_{1}}{v}\leq\mathds{1}_{V\geq 1}
and
\Delta v=\mathds{1}_{V\geq 1}\,\frac{\hbar^{2}}{v^{3}}\leq\mathds{1}_{V\geq 1}\,\frac{1}{\hbar}
, and so

(43)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{p}\cdot{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\boldsymbol{p}}\,e^{\frac{d_{1}}{\hbar}}\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{p}\cdot{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\boldsymbol{p}}\,e^{\frac{v}{\hbar}}\leq e\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,V_{-}\,.

Similarly as for the Agmon estimate, restricting to
\Omega_{1,R}^{c}
gives Inequality (
40
) and then using the inequalities between Schatten norms leads to Inequality (
41
). This inequality with
R=1
, combined with the fact that

(44)

\left|{\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right|^{2}={\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{2}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\leq{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\left(H+V_{-}\right){\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\leq 2\left\|{V_{-}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}

leads to Inequality (
42
).
∎

In the case of a vector valued function
u:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the previous lemma gives the following.

Corollary 2.6
.

Let
d\geq 1
. Then, there holds

\left\|{\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{1,R}^{c}}u\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\leq\frac{C_{d}}{R^{d/2}}\,e^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{R}{8\hbar}}\left\|{\left|{u}\right|^{2}\,e^{-\,\frac{d_{1}}{2\hbar}}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}^{1/2}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,V_{-}\right)^{1/2}

with
C_{d}=\left(2d/(\pi e)\right)^{d/2}
. Thus, if
\beta\,\hbar\leq 1/4
,

\left\|{u\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\leq C_{\beta,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\left\|{V_{-}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\left\|{\left|{u}\right|\,e^{-\beta\,d_{1}}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}.

with
C_{\beta,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}=\sqrt{2}\,e^{\beta}+C_{d}\,e^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{8\hbar}}\left\|{\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{L^{1}}^{1/2}
.

Proof.

Diagonalizing
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
in the form
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\sum_{j\geq 0}\ket{\psi_{j}}\bra{\psi_{j}}
with
(\psi_{j})_{j\geq 0}
orthonormal, we can write

\left\|{u\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}^{2}=h^{d}\tr\!\left({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\boldsymbol{p}\cdot u\,u\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)=h^{d}\sum_{j\geq 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|{u\cdot\hbar\nabla\psi_{j}}\right|^{2}\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|{u}\right|^{2}h^{d}\sum_{j\geq 0}\left|{\hbar\nabla\psi_{j}}\right|^{2}

hence by Formula (
37
),

\left\|{\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{1,R}^{c}}u\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq\int_{\Omega_{1,R}^{c}}\left|{u}\right|^{2}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{p}\cdot{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\boldsymbol{p}}\,,

which yields the result as in the previous proof using the inequality between Schatten norms and Inequality (
40
).
∎

Let us give as an application of the Agmon-like estimates for gradients a lemma on the decay in the momentum direction for
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
. These will find an application when we treat Coulomb-like potentials.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
d\geq 1
, and assume that
\nabla V
and
\Delta V
are locally integrable. Then,

\left\|{\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{4}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\leq C_{\beta,V,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\left(1+\left\|{V_{-}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)

with
C_{\beta,V,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}=\left\|{V_{-}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}+3\left\|{V{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}+2\,C_{\beta,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\left\|{\left|{\hbar\nabla V}\right|e^{-\beta\,d_{1}}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}+\left\|{\hbar^{2}\Delta V{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}
.

Proof.

Since
\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{4}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{2}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,H-\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{2}V{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
, and
\left[{\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{2},V}\right]=-i\hbar\left(\boldsymbol{p}\cdot\nabla V+\nabla V\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\right)
, it follows that

\displaystyle\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{4}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}

\displaystyle=\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{2}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,H-V\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{2}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}+i\hbar\left(\boldsymbol{p}\cdot\nabla V+\nabla V\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\right){\boldsymbol{\gamma}}

\displaystyle=\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{2}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,H-V{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,H+V^{2}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}+2i\hbar\,\nabla V\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}+\hbar^{2}\Delta V{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}

and so taking the operator norm, we deduce from the triangle inequality that

\left\|{\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{4}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\leq I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}

with

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=\left\|{\left(\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{2}-V\right){\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,H}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\leq\left\|{\left(H-2V\right){\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\left\|{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,H}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|{\left(V^{2}+\hbar^{2}\Delta V\right){\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\leq\left\|{V_{-}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\left\|{V{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}+\hbar^{2}\left\|{\Delta V{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}

\displaystyle I_{3}

\displaystyle=2\hbar\left\|{\nabla V\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}.

The term
I_{1}
is bounded using
H_{-}\leq\left\|{V_{-}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}
by

I_{1}\leq\left(\left\|{V_{-}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}+2\left\|{V{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\right)\left\|{V_{-}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}

while the terms
I_{2}
and
I_{3}
are bounded using the Agmon-like inequalities in Proposition
2.4
and Corollary
2.6
.
∎

Lemma 2.8

(Agmon-like estimate for the Hessian)
.

In the weak sense, it holds

(45)

0\leq\varrho_{\boldsymbol{p}^{2}:{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\boldsymbol{p}^{2}}\leq\left(\tfrac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\Delta-V\right)\varrho_{\boldsymbol{p}\cdot{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\boldsymbol{p}}-\tfrac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\,\nabla\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\cdot\nabla V\,.

Combined with the previous Agmon-like estimates, it gives for
\beta\,\hbar\leq 1/8

\left\|{e^{\beta d_{1}}\boldsymbol{p}^{2}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\leq e^{\beta}\left(\left\|{V_{-}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}+\left\|{V{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\right)+C_{d}\,C_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}},V}^{1/2}\left\|{\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{L^{1}}^{1/2}

with
C_{d}=\left(2d/(\pi e)\right)^{d/2}
and

(46)

C_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}},V}=\left\|{\left(\tfrac{\hbar}{2}\left|{\nabla V}\right|+\hbar^{2}\Delta V\right)e^{-\frac{d_{1}}{2\hbar}}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\left(1+\left\|{V_{-}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)+\sqrt{e}\left\|{V_{-}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}.

Remark 2.3.1
.

As in Corollary
2.6
, this allows to control terms of the form
\left\|{u\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\,\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}
if
u:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
or
\left\|{u:\boldsymbol{p}^{2}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}
if
u
is matrix valued.

Proof.

Let
\varphi\geq 0
. Then since
\left[{\boldsymbol{p},\varphi}\right]=-i\hbar\nabla\varphi
, with
\nabla\varphi
the operator of multiplication by
\nabla\varphi
, and by cyclicity of the trace

\displaystyle 0\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{p}^{2}:{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\boldsymbol{p}^{2}}\,\varphi

\displaystyle=h^{d}\tr\!\left(-i\hbar\,\boldsymbol{p}\cdot{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\boldsymbol{p}\left(\nabla\varphi\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\right)+\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{2}\boldsymbol{p}\cdot{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\boldsymbol{p}\,\varphi\right)

\displaystyle=h^{d}\tr\!\left(-i\hbar\,\boldsymbol{p}\cdot{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\boldsymbol{p}\left(\nabla\varphi\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\right)+\boldsymbol{p}\cdot H{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\boldsymbol{p}\,\varphi-\boldsymbol{p}\cdot V{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\boldsymbol{p}\,\varphi\right)

which can be written

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{p}^{2}:{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\boldsymbol{p}^{2}}\,\varphi=h^{d}\tr\!\left(-i\hbar\,\boldsymbol{p}\cdot{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\boldsymbol{p}\left(\nabla\varphi\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\right)+\boldsymbol{p}\cdot H{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\boldsymbol{p}\,\varphi-V\boldsymbol{p}\cdot{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\boldsymbol{p}\,\varphi+i\hbar\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\boldsymbol{p}\cdot\nabla V\varphi\right)\\
=h^{d}\tr\!\left(\boldsymbol{p}\cdot H{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\boldsymbol{p}\,\varphi+i\hbar\left({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\boldsymbol{p}\cdot\left(\nabla V\right)\varphi-\boldsymbol{p}\cdot{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\boldsymbol{p}\left(\nabla\varphi\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\right)\right)\right)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{p}\cdot{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\boldsymbol{p}}\,V\varphi\,.

Taking the real part and observing that
i\hbar\,\boldsymbol{p}\cdot\nabla\varphi-i\hbar\,\nabla\varphi\cdot\boldsymbol{p}=\hbar^{2}\Delta\varphi
yields

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{p}^{2}:{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\boldsymbol{p}^{2}}\,\varphi=h^{d}\tr\!\left(\boldsymbol{p}\cdot H{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\boldsymbol{p}\,\varphi\right)+\tfrac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\nabla\cdot\left(\varphi\nabla V\right)+\varrho_{\boldsymbol{p}\cdot{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\boldsymbol{p}}\,\Delta\varphi-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{p}\cdot{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\boldsymbol{p}}\,V\varphi

and we conclude using the fact that
\varphi\geq 0
and
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,H\leq 0
that

0\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{p}^{2}:{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\boldsymbol{p}^{2}}\,\varphi\leq\tfrac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\nabla\cdot\left(\varphi\nabla V\right)+\varrho_{\boldsymbol{p}\cdot{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\boldsymbol{p}}\left(\tfrac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\Delta\varphi-V\varphi\right)

which gives Inequality (
45
). Let
v=(\hbar^{2}+d_{1}^{2})^{1/2}
and take
\varphi=e^{\frac{v}{2\hbar}}
. Then we get similarly as for the Agmon estimate for gradients

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{p}^{2}:{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\boldsymbol{p}^{2}}\,e^{\frac{v}{2\hbar}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\tfrac{\hbar}{4}\,\nabla v\cdot\nabla V+\tfrac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\Delta V\right)\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,e^{\frac{v}{2\hbar}}+\left(\tfrac{\hbar}{4}\,\Delta v+\tfrac{1}{8}\left|{\nabla v}\right|^{2}-V\right)\varrho_{\boldsymbol{p}\cdot{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\boldsymbol{p}}\,e^{\frac{v}{2\hbar}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{e}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\tfrac{\hbar}{4}\left|{\nabla V}\right|+\tfrac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\Delta V\right)\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,e^{\frac{d_{1}}{2\hbar}}+V_{-}\,\varrho_{\boldsymbol{p}\cdot{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\boldsymbol{p}}

where we used the fact that
v\leq\hbar+d_{1}
to get the last inequality. By Inequality (
36
) and using
\tr\!\left({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{2}\right)\leq\tr\!\left({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,V_{-}\right)
, it follows that

(47)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{p}^{2}:{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\boldsymbol{p}^{2}}\,e^{\frac{d_{1}}{2\hbar}}\leq C_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}},V}\left\|{\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{L^{1}}.

Thus, arguing as before, and using the embedding between Schatten norms, the fact that
\lVert\mathds{1}_{\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|\neq 0}\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{-2}\boldsymbol{p}^{2}\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\leq 1
, it gives

\left\|{e^{\beta d_{1}}\boldsymbol{p}^{2}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\leq C_{d}\,C_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}},V}^{1/2}+e^{\beta}\left\|{\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{2}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}

and the result follows as in the proof of Lemma
2.1
.
∎

Lemma 2.9
.

In
d\geq 1
, assume that
V
,
\nabla V
,
\nabla^{2}V
,
\nabla\Delta V
,
\Delta^{2}V
are locally bounded. Then,

\left\|{\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{6}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\leq\widetilde{C}_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}},V}\left(1+\left\|{\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{4}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\right)\left(1+\left\|{V_{-}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)

with

\widetilde{C}_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}},V}=2+2\left\|{\left|{V}\right|^{3}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}+\hbar\left(4\left\|{\nabla V\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}+3\left\|{\nabla(V^{2})\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\right)\\
+\hbar^{2}\left(2\left\|{\Delta V{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}+3\left\|{V\Delta V\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}+4\left\|{\nabla^{2}V:\boldsymbol{p}^{2}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}+4\left\|{\left|{\nabla V}\right|^{2}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\right)\\
+4\,\hbar^{3}\left\|{\Delta\nabla V\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}+\hbar^{4}\left\|{\Delta^{2}V\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}.

Proof.

We write
\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{6}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{4}H{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{4}V{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
and then since
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{2}={\boldsymbol{\gamma}}

\left\|{\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{4}H{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}=\left\|{\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{4}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,H}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\leq\left\|{\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{4}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\left\|{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,H}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}

while by the chain rule

\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{4}V{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\hbar^{4}\Delta^{2}V\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}+4i\hbar^{3}\Delta\nabla V\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}+2\hbar^{2}\Delta V\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{2}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}+4\hbar^{2}\,\nabla^{2}V:\boldsymbol{p}^{2}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\\
+4i\hbar\,\nabla V\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{2}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}+V\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{4}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,.

Observe that we can again use the fact that
\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{2}=H-V
for some terms. This gives for instance

\left\|{\Delta V\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{2}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\leq\left\|{\Delta V{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\left\|{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,H}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}+\left\|{\Delta V\,V\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}

and

\displaystyle\nabla V\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{2}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}

\displaystyle=\nabla V\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,H-\nabla V\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\,V{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}

\displaystyle=\nabla V\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,H-V\nabla V\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}+i\hbar\left|{\nabla V}\right|^{2}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}

and, using also the identity
\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{2}V=V\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{2}-2i\hbar\,\nabla V\cdot\boldsymbol{p}-\hbar^{2}\Delta V
,

\displaystyle V\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{4}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}

\displaystyle=V\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{2}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,H-V\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{2}V{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}

\displaystyle=V{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,H^{2}-2\,V^{2}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,H-V^{3}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}+i\hbar\,\nabla V^{2}\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}+\hbar^{2}\,V\Delta V{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,.

The result follows by using the triangle inequality for the operator norm in all these inequalities, combining them, and using each time that
\left\|{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,H}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\leq\left\|{V_{-}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}
.
∎

3.
Commutator estimates

The main purpose of this section is the proof of the commutators estimates in Theorem
1.1
. We assume the interaction potential satisfies the assumptions contained in its statement.

3.1.
Motivation

In order to motivate the upcoming methods, let us consider the simplest case of the one-dimensional harmonic oscillator
H=-\hbar^{2}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{2}}+x^{2}
. As is well-known its spectrum consists of the sequence of eigenvalues
E_{n}=\left(2n+1\right)\hbar
for numbers
n\geq 0
. Given a chemical potential
\mu\in\hbar\,\mathbb{N}
, let us denote

{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}:=\sum_{E_{n}\leq\mu}\ket{\varphi_{n}}\bra{\varphi_{n}}

where
\left(\varphi_{n}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
are the Hermite functions at scale
\hbar
.

We now claim that the commutator estimates follow as soon as we know that the eigenvalues satisfy the
gap condition

(48)

\forall n,k\geq 1,\,\left|{E_{n}-E_{k}}\right|\geq C\,\hbar\left|{n-k}\right|.

Indeed, let us consider now a self-adjoint operator
A
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, which we think of either being
A=x
or
A=-i\hbar\,\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x}
. For the purpose of the exposition, let us work with the Hilbert–Schmidt norms which are easier to compute. Indeed, using the fact that
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{2}={\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
, we find

\frac{1}{4}\tr\!\left(\left|{\left[{A,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right]}\right|^{2}\right)=\tr\!\left(\left|{\left(\mathbf{1}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)A\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right|^{2}\right)=\sum_{E_{k}>\mu}\sum_{E_{n}\leq\mu}\left|{\Braket{\varphi_{k}|A\varphi_{n}}}\right|^{2}.

Of course, if
A
was not present we readily obtain
\Braket{\varphi_{n}|\varphi_{k}}=0
due to summation constrains. However, the operator
A
induces correlations between the different eigenfunctions. These correlations, in fact, decay with
\left|{n-k}\right|
thanks to Inequality (
48
). In order to estimate these correlations we may easily compute using the fact that
\varphi_{n}
are eigenfunctions of
H

\left|{\Braket{\varphi_{k}|A\varphi_{n}}}\right|^{2}=\frac{\left|{\Braket{\varphi_{k}|\left[{H,A}\right]\varphi_{n}}}\right|^{2}}{\left|{E_{k}-E_{n}}\right|^{2}}\leq\frac{C\,\hbar^{-2}}{\left|{k-n}\right|^{2}}\left|{\Braket{\varphi_{k}|\left[{H,A}\right]\varphi_{n}}}\right|^{2}.

Here, we have used the gap condition to obtain some decay with respect to
n
and
k
. Additionally, thanks to the separation
E_{n}\leq\mu<E_{k}
we have
\left|{k-n}\right|\geq\left|{k_{0}-n}\right|
for some fixed
k_{0}\leq k
. The Cauchy–Schwarz inequality now implies

\sum_{E_{k}>\mu}\sum_{E_{n}\leq\mu}\left|{\Braket{\varphi_{k}|A\varphi_{n}}}\right|^{2}\leq C\,\hbar^{-2}\sum_{E_{n}\leq\mu}\frac{\left\|{\left[{H,A}\right]\varphi_{n}}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}{\left|{k_{0}-n}\right|^{2}}\,.

Finally, one may verify that for each eigenfunction
\varphi_{n}
with
E_{n}\leq\mu
we have
\left\|{\left[{H,A}\right]\varphi_{n}}\right\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\,\hbar\,\mu
. All in all, we obtain

(49)

\tr\!\left(\left|{\left[{A,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right]}\right|^{2}\right)\leq C\sum_{E_{n}\leq\mu}\frac{\mu^{2}}{\left|{k_{0}-n}\right|^{2}}\,.

Observe now that the sum on the right-hand side is convergent and uniformly bounded in
\hbar
. On the other hand, in one dimension
\tr\!\left({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{2}\right)=C\,\hbar^{-1}
and we have, therefore, reduced by a factor
\hbar
the growth of the commutator. In other words, (
49
) coincides with (
11
) for
p=2
and
d=1
.

The argument presented above can be readily generalized to the harmonic oscillator in any spatial dimension, as we know that the gap condition (
48
) is verified for such models. Additionally, it can be suitably modified to prove its stronger trace-class variant.

For more complicated models, we do not expect such strong gap condition to hold pointwise (i.e. for all
n
and
k
) but at least in an
averaged
sense. More precisely, we replace it with the resolvent inequality (see Lemma
3.2
)

(50)

h^{d}\tr\!\left(\mathds{1}_{H\leq 0}\left(\hbar-H\right)^{-2}\right)\leq C\,\hbar\,.

Similar to (
48
), the new bound (
50
) states that there is an averaged separation of eigenvalues by a gap of order
\hbar
. As we shall see, it will become sufficient to prove our commutator estimates, which is a suitable modification of the argument just presented.

3.2.
Eigenvalue estimates
