As explained in the introductory section our starting point is the optimal Weyl law for
C^{1,\alpha}
potentials. More precisely, it follows from
[
47
, Theorem 1.5]
that for potentials
V
verifying (
H1
) in
d\geq 3
, there exists
\mathcal{C}_{0}>0
such that for all
\hbar\in(0,1)

(51)

\left|{h^{d}\tr\!\left(\mathds{1}_{H\leq 0}\right)-\int_{\left|{\xi}\right|^{2}+V(x)\leq 0}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi}\right|\leq\mathcal{C}_{0}\,\hbar\,.

For the next observation, fix some
\nu>0
. Although not explicitly mentioned in
[
47
]
,
the constant
\mathcal{C}_{0}
is uniform over potentials which satisfy

(52)

\left\|{V}\right\|_{C^{1,\alpha}(\Omega_{\nu})}<C_{\Omega_{\nu}},\quad\text{ with }\quad\Omega_{\nu}=\{V\leq\nu\}\,,

where thanks to (
H1
) the set
\Omega_{\nu}
is compact. Indeed, the proof introduces a regularization
V_{\varepsilon}(x)
whose various
C^{k,\alpha}
semi-norms depend on
V
only through (
52
). The proof of the Weyl law is then a delicate pseudo-differential operators expansion based on the regularization
V_{\varepsilon}
. As stated in
[
47
]
, all resulting estimates depend only on the
C^{k,\alpha}
norms of
V_{\varepsilon}
, which then depend on
V
only through
C_{\Omega_{\nu}}
.

This observation has the following consequence. Namely, one can find a small enough and fixed
0<\varepsilon_{0}\leq 1
(independent of
\hbar
) such that the bound (
52
) still holds if we replace
V\mapsto V+E
for all
\left|{E}\right|\leq\varepsilon_{0}
. Thus, the following perturbed version of the result of
[
47
]
is valid

(53)

\left|{h^{d}\tr\!\left(\mathds{1}_{H\leq E}\right)-\int_{\left|{\xi}\right|^{2}+V(x)\leq E}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi}\right|\leq\mathcal{C}_{0}\,\hbar\,,

for all
E\in[-\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{0}]
, where
\mathcal{C}_{0}>0
is the same constant as before.

In our setting, the main consequence of (
53
) is the validity of the following
local eigenvalue estimate
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
a\in[-\varepsilon_{0},0]
and
b\in[a,1]
. Then, in
d\geq 3

(54)

h^{d}\tr{\mathds{1}_{[a,b]}(H)}\leq\mathcal{C}_{1}\left(\left|{b-a}\right|+\hbar\right)

where
\mathcal{C}_{1}=2\,\mathcal{C}_{0}+\left(\frac{\omega_{d}}{2}+\frac{\mathsf{L}_{0,d}}{\varepsilon_{0}}\right)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(V-1\right)_{-}^{d/2-1}
.

Proof.

Assume first
b\leq\varepsilon_{0}
so that
-\varepsilon_{0}\leq a\leq b\leq\varepsilon_{0}
. Since
\mathds{1}_{[a,b]}(H)=\mathds{1}_{H\leq b}-\mathds{1}_{H<a}
, Inequality (
53
) gives

h^{d}\tr{\mathds{1}_{[a,b]}(H)}\leq 2\,\mathcal{C}_{0}\,\hbar+\frac{\omega_{d}}{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(b-V\right)_{+}^{\frac{d}{2}}-\left(a-V\right)_{+}^{\frac{d}{2}}.

The second term on the right-hand side is now bounded using the fact that for
0\leq\alpha\leq\beta
,
\beta_{+}^{d/2}-\alpha_{+}^{d/2}\leq\frac{d}{2}\left(\beta-\alpha\right)\beta_{+}^{d/2-1}
, which gives

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(b-V\right)_{+}^{\frac{d}{2}}-\left(a-V\right)_{+}^{\frac{d}{2}}\leq\frac{d}{2}\left(b-a\right)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(b-V\right)_{+}^{\frac{d}{2}-1}

which gives the result in this case. Assume now
\varepsilon_{0}\leq b\leq 1
so that
\left|{b-a}\right|\geq\varepsilon_{0}
. Then, it suffices to use the Cwikel–Lieb–Rozenblum inequality (
25
) to find

h^{d}\tr{\mathds{1}_{[a,b]}(H)}\leq h^{d}\tr\!\left(\mathds{1}_{H\leq b}\right)\leq\frac{\mathsf{L}_{0,d}}{\varepsilon_{0}}\left|{b-a}\right|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(b-V\right)_{+}^{d/2}.

This finishes the proof.
∎

We will use the local eigenvalue estimate both directly in the proof of the commutator estimates, but also to establish the following singular sum estimate. Here and below,
\lambda
is an auxiliary parameter, which
we take as
\lambda=\hbar
in some cases of interest. However, keeping
\lambda
flexible allows us to improve upon some rates, as well as proving Besov-type estimates.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
d\geq 3
. Then for any
\lambda\geq\hbar

\displaystyle h^{d}\tr\!\left(\mathds{1}_{H\leq 0}\left(\lambda-H\right)^{-2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{C}_{2}\,\lambda^{-1}

\displaystyle h^{d}\tr\!\left(\mathds{1}_{H\leq 0}\left(\lambda-H\right)^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{C}_{2}\left(1+\ln\!\left(1+\tfrac{\varepsilon_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\right)

where
\mathcal{C}_{2}=6\,\mathcal{C}_{1}+\frac{\mathsf{L}_{0,d}}{\varepsilon_{0}}\int V_{-}^{d/2}
.

Remark 3.2.1
.

Essentially, in the proof we compare the trace with the Riemann sum corresponding to the integral
\int_{-\infty}^{0}(\lambda-u)^{-2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u=\lambda^{-1}
.

Proof of Lemma
3.2
.

We will only give the detailed proof of the first item. The proof of the second one is completely analogous. Observe first that when
\lambda>\varepsilon_{0}
, we can just use the fact that
\left(\lambda-H\right)^{-2}\leq\lambda^{-2}\leq\left(\varepsilon_{0}\,\lambda\right)^{-1}
and the Cwikel–Lieb–Rozenblum bound (
25
) to get

h^{d}\tr\!\left(\mathds{1}_{H\leq 0}\left(\lambda-H\right)^{-2}\right)\leq\frac{\mathsf{L}_{0,d}}{\varepsilon_{0}\,\lambda}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}V_{-}^{d/2}.

Now we assume
\lambda\leq\varepsilon_{0}
and we decompose
(-\infty,0]=(-\infty,-\varepsilon_{0}]\cup(-\varepsilon_{0},0]
. On the first region, we can again use the Cwikel–Lieb–Rozenblum bound to get

h^{d}\tr\!\left(\mathds{1}_{H\leq-\varepsilon_{0}}\left(\lambda-H\right)^{-2}\right)\leq\frac{h^{d}}{\left(\lambda+\varepsilon_{0}\right)^{2}}\tr\!\left(\mathds{1}_{H\leq-\varepsilon_{0}}\right)\leq\frac{\mathsf{L}_{0,d}}{3\,\lambda\,\varepsilon_{0}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}V_{-}^{d/2}.

We now focus on the interval
(-\varepsilon_{0},0]
. To this end, consider an integer
n\geq 1
, soon to be chosen (the reader should think
n\sim\lambda^{-1}
), and let us partition the interval
[-\varepsilon_{0},0]
into
n
disjoint intervals of equal length
\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{n}
to obtain

\mathds{1}_{(-\varepsilon_{0},0]}(H)=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\mathds{1}_{(-\frac{j+1}{n}\,\varepsilon_{0},-\frac{j}{n}\,\varepsilon_{0}]}(H)\,.

Note that the map
r\mapsto(\lambda-r)^{-2}
is increasing on
\mathbb{R}_{-}
. Hence, we can estimate
H\leq-\frac{j}{n}\,\varepsilon_{0}
over each interval

\tr\!\left(\mathds{1}_{(-\varepsilon_{0},0]}(H)\left(\lambda-H\right)^{-2}\right)\leq\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\frac{1}{\left(\lambda+\frac{j}{n}\,\varepsilon_{0}\right)^{2}}\tr\!\left(\mathds{1}_{(-\frac{j+1}{n}\,\varepsilon_{0},-\frac{j}{n}\,\varepsilon_{0}]}(H)\right).

Next, we use the local eigenvalue estimate (
54
) to
estimate the traces. We obtain

h^{d}\tr\!\left(\mathds{1}_{(-\varepsilon_{0},0]}(H)\left(\lambda-H\right)^{-2}\right)\leq\mathcal{C}_{1}\left(\varepsilon_{0}+n\hbar\right)\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\frac{1}{\left(\lambda+\frac{j}{n}\,\varepsilon_{0}\right)^{2}}

and we estimate the Riemann sum via an integral comparison using the fact that
r\mapsto(\lambda+r)^{-2}
is decreasing. This gives, thanks to
\lambda\geq 0

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\frac{1}{\left(\lambda+\frac{j}{n}\,\varepsilon_{0}\right)^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{n\,\lambda^{2}}+\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r}{\left(\lambda+\varepsilon_{0}\,r\right)^{2}}=\frac{1}{n\,\lambda^{2}}+\frac{1}{\lambda\left(\lambda+\varepsilon_{0}\right)}\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\left(\frac{1}{n\lambda}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{0}}\right).

We now choose an integer
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\varepsilon_{0}\leq n\lambda\leq 2\,\varepsilon_{0}
and use
\hbar\leq\lambda
to find

h^{d}\tr\!\left(\mathds{1}_{(-\varepsilon_{0},0]}(H)\left(\lambda-H\right)^{-2}\right)\leq\frac{\mathcal{C}_{1}}{\lambda}\left(\varepsilon_{0}+n\lambda\right)\left(\frac{1}{n\lambda}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{0}}\right)\leq 6\,\mathcal{C}_{1}\,.

This finishes the proof.
∎

3.3.
General Hilbert–Schmidt bounds

For the proof of Theorem
1.1
we will develop general commutator estimates for characteristic functions of self-adjoint operators. In particular, they provide bounds for
\left[{A,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right]
in terms of more tractable objects. See Proposition
3.3
and
3.4
for Hilbert–Schmidt and trace-class bounds. Additionally, we will need to prove more technical counterparts in Proposition
3.5
and Proposition
5.3
.

Proposition 3.3

(Hilbert–Schmidt bounds)
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\mathds{1}_{H\leq 0}
with
H=-\hbar^{2}\Delta+V
in
d\geq 3
, and let
A
be a normal operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then, for all
\lambda\geq\hbar

(55)

h^{d}\tr\!\left(\left|{\left[{A,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right]}\right|^{2}\right)\leq\mathcal{C}_{\textnormal{HS}}\left(\lambda\left\|{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,A}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}^{2}+\frac{1}{\lambda}\left\|{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\left[{A,H}\right]\left(\mathbf{1}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\right)

where
\mathcal{C}_{\textnormal{HS}}=2\left(\mathcal{C}_{1}+\mathcal{C}_{2}\right)
.

Remark 3.3.1
.

Taking
\lambda=\hbar
and either
A=x
or
A=\boldsymbol{p}=-i\hbar\nabla
, then
\lambda\left\|{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,A}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}^{2}
becomes
O(\hbar)
while for the second terms one can use
0\leq{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\leq\mathbf{1}
and the fact that

\left\|{\left[{H,x}\right]{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\leq 2\,\hbar^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\quad\text{ and }\quad\left\|{\left[{H,\boldsymbol{p}}\right]{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\leq\hbar^{2}\left\|{\nabla V{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\,.

Thus, the right-hand side of Inequality (
55
) becomes
O(\hbar)
once we can control
\left\|{\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}
,
\left\|{x\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}
and
\left\|{\nabla V\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}
uniformly in
\hbar
.

Remark 3.3.2
.

For the proof, we establish the more general inequality: for all
\lambda>0
and
d\geq 1

(56)

\tr\!\left(\left|{\left[{A,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right]}\right|^{2}\right)\leq 2\tr\!\left(\mathds{1}_{0<H<\lambda}\left|{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,A}\right|^{2}\right)+2\left\|{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\left[{A,H}\right]\left(\mathbf{1}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\tr\!\left({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\left(\lambda-H\right)^{-2}\right).

This bound can be formulated in an abstract setting. Namely, the bound is also valid for a sequence of self-adjoint operators
(H_{\hbar})_{\hbar\in(0,1)}
on a Hilbert space
\mathscr{H}
. See Appendix
A
.

In the following proof, we rely on the fact that
H
has discrete spectrum and prove the intermediate inequality (
56
) using an eigenbasis expansion. The Hilbert–Schmidt estimate (
56
) can be established without requiring that
H
has discrete spectrum; see Appendix
A
for an alternative proof. Currently, we cannot adapt that alternative approach to prove the corresponding trace-class bounds in Subsection
3.4
. Thus, we include here the proof with an eigenbasis expansion as it will also set up the stage for the upcoming trace-class bounds.

Proof of Proposition
3.3
.

First, observe that using
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\left(\mathbf{1}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)=0
, the fact that
A
is normal and the cyclicity of the trace gives

\tr\!\left(\left|{\left[{A,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right]}\right|^{2}\right)=2\tr\!\left(A^{*}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,A\left(\mathbf{1}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)\right).

Decomposing
\mathbf{1}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\mathds{1}_{0<H<\lambda}+\mathds{1}_{H\geq\lambda}
and using the fact that
A^{*}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,A=\left|{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,A}\right|^{2}
, this can be written

(57)

\tr\!\left(\left|{\left[{A,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right]}\right|^{2}\right)=2\tr\!\left(\left|{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,A}\right|^{2}\mathds{1}_{0<H<\lambda}\right)+2\tr\!\left(A^{*}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,A\,\mathds{1}_{H\geq\lambda}\right).

On the right-hand side, the first term is already the first term of Equation (
56
). We now proceed to estimate the second one. To this end, we recall that the spectrum of
H
is discrete and given by an increasing sequence of eigenvalues
(\lambda_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}\subset\mathbb{R}
. Thus,

{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\sum_{\lambda_{k}\leq 0}\mathbf{P}_{k}\quad\text{ where }\quad\mathbf{P}_{k}=\mathds{1}_{H=\lambda_{k}}\,,

which, using also the cyclicity of the trace, yields

(58)

\tr\!\left(A^{*}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,A\,\mathds{1}_{H\geq\lambda}\right)=\sum_{\lambda_{k}\geq\lambda}\sum_{\lambda_{j}<0}\tr\!\left(\mathbf{P}_{k}\,A^{*}\,\mathbf{P}_{j}\,A\,\mathbf{P}_{k}\right).

Next, observe that by the definition of the projectors
\mathbf{P}
,

(59)

\mathbf{P}_{j}\,A\,\mathbf{P}_{k}=\frac{\mathbf{P}_{j}\left[{A,H}\right]\mathbf{P}_{k}}{\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{j}}

from which it follows that, bounding
(\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{j})^{-2}\leq(\lambda-\lambda_{j})^{-2}
,

\displaystyle\tr\!\left(A^{*}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,A\lx@nobreakspace\,\mathds{1}_{H\geq\lambda}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda_{j}<0}\sum_{\lambda_{k}\geq\lambda}\frac{1}{(\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{j})^{2}}\tr\!\left(\mathbf{P}_{k}\left[{A,H}\right]^{*}\mathbf{P}_{j}\left[{A,H}\right]\mathbf{P}_{k}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\lambda_{j}<0}\sum_{\lambda_{k}\geq\lambda}\frac{1}{(\lambda-\lambda_{j})^{2}}\tr\!\left(\mathbf{P}_{k}\left[{A,H}\right]^{*}\mathbf{P}_{j}\left[{A,H}\right]\mathbf{P}_{k}\right).

By cyclicity of the trace and by writing
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\left(\lambda-H\right)^{-2}
and
\mathds{1}_{H\geq\lambda}
in terms of the projectors
\mathbf{P}
, this can be written

\tr\!\left(A^{*}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,A\lx@nobreakspace\,\mathds{1}_{H\geq\lambda}\right)\leq\tr\!\left(\mathds{1}_{H\geq\lambda}\left[{A,H}\right]^{*}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\left(\lambda-H\right)^{-2}\left[{A,H}\right]\mathds{1}_{H\geq\lambda}\right),

Using
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}={\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{2}
twice, the operator inequality
\mathds{1}_{H\geq\lambda}\leq\mathbf{1}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
, and the Hölder inequality for the trace yields

\displaystyle\tr\!\left(A^{*}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,A\lx@nobreakspace\,\mathds{1}_{H\geq\lambda}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\left[{A,H}\right]\left(\mathbf{1}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\tr\!\left({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\left(\lambda-H\right)^{-2}\right).

Plugging this estimate back in Inequality (
57
) proves Formula (
56
). Proposition
3.3
then follows for
\lambda\geq\hbar
thanks to Lemma
3.1
and Lemma
3.2
.
∎

3.4.
General trace-class bounds

For the case of the trace-class commutator estimates, we state two propositions. The first one will be useful for the position commutator estimates. The second one will be useful for the momentum estimates.

Proposition 3.4

(First trace-class bounds)
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\mathds{1}_{H\leq 0}
with
H=-\hbar^{2}\Delta+V
in
d\geq 3
and let
A
be a normal operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then, for all
\lambda\geq\hbar

\displaystyle h^{d}\tr\!\left(\left|{\left[{A,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right]}\right|\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{C}_{\textnormal{Tr}}\left(\lambda\left\|{A\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}+\frac{1}{\lambda}\left\|{\left(\mathbf{1}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)\left[{\left[{A,H}\right],H}\right]{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\right)

\displaystyle h^{d}\tr\!\left(\left|{\left[{A,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right]}\right|\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{C}_{\textnormal{Tr}}\left(\lambda\left\|{A\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}+\left(1+\ln\!\left(1+\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\right)\left\|{(\mathbf{1}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})\left[{A,H}\right]{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\right),

where
\mathcal{C}_{\rm Tr}=2\left(\mathcal{C}_{1}+\mathcal{C}_{2}\right)
.

Remark 3.4.1
.

Again, in the case
\lambda=\hbar
, the first term in the inequalities is
O(\hbar)
and, using
0\leq{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\leq\mathbf{1}
, we obtain for the second terms

\displaystyle\left\|{\left[{\left[{x,H}\right],H}\right]{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\,\hbar^{2}\left\|{\nabla V\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\left\|{\left[{\left[{\boldsymbol{p},H}\right],H}\right]{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq\hbar^{2}\left\|{\left(\boldsymbol{p}\cdot\nabla^{2}V+\nabla^{2}V\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\right){\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\left\|{\left[{\boldsymbol{p},H}\right]{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq\hbar\left\|{\nabla V\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\,.

The first bound gives optimal commutators estimates for
A=x
. The second bound with
A=\boldsymbol{p}
can be bounded using
\boldsymbol{p}\cdot\nabla^{2}V=\nabla^{2}V\cdot\boldsymbol{p}-i\hbar\,\nabla\Delta V
, but this involves
3
gradients on
V
. See Proposition
3.5
for an alternative. On the other hand, if
V
has only one derivative, the third bound with
A=\boldsymbol{p}
will still give a bound but with an additional
\left|{\ln\hbar}\right|
.

Remark 3.4.2
.

We will prove the general estimates: for all
\lambda>0
and
d\geq 1

(60)

\displaystyle\tr{\left|{\left[{A,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right]}\right|}

\displaystyle\leq 2\tr\!\left(\left|{\mathds{1}_{0<H<\lambda}\,A\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right|\right)+2\left\|{\left(\mathbf{1}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)\left[{\left[{A,H}\right],H}\right]{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\tr\!\left(\left(\lambda-H\right)^{-2}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)

(61)

\displaystyle\tr{\left|{\left[{A,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right]}\right|}

\displaystyle\leq 2\tr\!\left(\left|{\mathds{1}_{0<H<\lambda}\,A\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right|\right)+2\left\|{\left(\mathbf{1}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)\left[{A,H}\right]{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\tr\!\left(\left(\lambda-H\right)^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right).

As in the Hilbert–Schmidt case, these bounds can also be formulated in an abstract setting.

Proof.

For the proof let us write
\left\|{A}\right\|_{p}^{p}=\tr\!\left(\left|{A}\right|^{p}\right)
. We only prove (
60
) in detail as the second one is analogous. Similarly as in the proof for the Hilbert–Schmidt estimates, we write the commutator using the operator
\mathbf{1}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
as
\left[{A,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right]=\left(\mathbf{1}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)A\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-\left(\mathbf{1}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right){\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,A
and we write
\mathbf{1}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\mathds{1}_{0<H<\lambda}+\mathds{1}_{H\geq\lambda}
to get

\left\|{\left[{A,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right]}\right\|_{1}\leq 2\left\|{\left(\mathbf{1}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)A\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{1}\leq 2\left\|{\mathds{1}_{0<H<\lambda}\,A\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{1}+2\left\|{\mathds{1}_{H\geq\lambda}\,A\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{1}.

It remains to bound the second term on the right-hand side. To this end, we proceed similarly as in the proof of the Hilbert–Schmidt estimates (with the same notations) but take here two commutators. Let
{\boldsymbol{b}}
be a compact operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then, expanding the projection operators and using Identity (
59
) yields

(62)

\displaystyle\tr\!\left(\mathds{1}_{H\geq\lambda}\,A\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,{\boldsymbol{b}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda_{k}\geq\lambda}\sum_{\lambda_{j}\leq 0}\frac{1}{(\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{j})^{2}}\tr\!\left(\mathbf{P}_{k}\left[{\left[{A,H}\right],H}\right]\mathbf{P}_{j}\,{\boldsymbol{b}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda_{k}\geq\lambda}\sum_{\lambda_{j}\leq 0}\frac{1}{(\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{j})^{2}}\tr\!\left(\mathbf{P}_{k}\,{\boldsymbol{c}}^{*}\,\mathbf{P}_{j}\,{\boldsymbol{b}}\,\mathbf{P}_{k}\right)

where we now denote
{\boldsymbol{c}}:=\left[{\left[{A,H}\right],H}\right]^{*}
. First, we observe that thanks to the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality over the trace

(63)

\tr\!\left(\left|{\mathbf{P}_{k}\,{\boldsymbol{c}}^{*}\,\mathbf{P}_{j}\,{\boldsymbol{b}}}\right|\right)\leq\left(\tr\!\left(\mathbf{P}_{k}\,{\boldsymbol{c}}^{*}\,\mathbf{P}_{j}\,{\boldsymbol{c}}\,\mathbf{P}_{k}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\tr\!\left(\mathbf{P}_{k}\,{\boldsymbol{b}}^{*}\,\mathbf{P}_{j}\,{\boldsymbol{b}}\,\mathbf{P}_{k}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Next, we bound the denominator using
\lambda_{k}\geq\lambda
in Identity (
62
), exchange the sums, use the bound (
63
), and the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality over
\lambda_{k}\in\sigma(H)
to obtain

\displaystyle\left|{\tr\!\left(\mathds{1}_{H\geq\lambda}\,A\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,{\boldsymbol{b}}\right)}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\lambda_{j}\leq 0}\frac{1}{\left(\lambda-\lambda_{j}\right)^{2}}\sum_{\lambda_{k}\geq\lambda}\left(\tr\!\left(\mathbf{P}_{k}\,{\boldsymbol{c}}^{*}\,\mathbf{P}_{j}\,{\boldsymbol{c}}\,\mathbf{P}_{k}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\tr\!\left(\mathbf{P}_{k}\,{\boldsymbol{b}}^{*}\,\mathbf{P}_{j}\,{\boldsymbol{b}}\,\mathbf{P}_{k}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\lambda_{j}\leq 0}\frac{1}{\left(\lambda-\lambda_{j}\right)^{2}}\left(\tr\!\left({\boldsymbol{c}}^{*}\,\mathbf{P}_{j}{\boldsymbol{c}}\,\mathds{1}_{H\geq\lambda}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\tr\!\left({\boldsymbol{b}}^{*}\,\mathbf{P}_{j}\,{\boldsymbol{b}}\,\mathds{1}_{H\geq\lambda}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Finally, we distribute one power
(\lambda-\lambda_{j})^{-2}
in each factor and do the Cauchy–Schwarz over
\lambda_{j}\in\sigma(H)
to get

\displaystyle\left|{\tr\!\left(\mathds{1}_{H\geq\lambda}\,A\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,{\boldsymbol{b}}\right)}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\tr\!\left({\boldsymbol{c}}^{*}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\left(\lambda-H\right)^{-2}{\boldsymbol{c}}\,\mathds{1}_{H\geq\lambda}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\tr\!\left({\boldsymbol{b}}^{*}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\left(\lambda-H\right)^{-2}{\boldsymbol{b}}\,\mathds{1}_{H\geq\lambda}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\mathds{1}_{H\geq\lambda}\left[{\left[{A,H}\right],H}\right]{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\left(\lambda-H\right)^{-1}}\right\|_{2}\left\|{\mathds{1}_{H\geq\lambda}\,{\boldsymbol{b}}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\left(\lambda-H\right)^{-1}}\right\|_{2}.

The two factors can now be bounded in the following way

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathds{1}_{H\geq\lambda}\,{\boldsymbol{b}}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\left(\lambda-H\right)^{-1}}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{{\boldsymbol{b}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\lVert{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\left(\lambda-H\right)^{-1}\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathds{1}_{H\geq\lambda}\left[{\left[{A,H}\right],H}\right]{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\left(\lambda-H\right)^{-1}}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\left[{\left[{A,H}\right],H}\right]\left(\mathbf{1}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\lVert{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\left(\lambda-H\right)^{-1}\rVert_{2}\,.

Observe that
\lVert{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\left(\lambda-H\right)^{-1}\rVert_{2}^{2}=\tr{\left(\lambda-H\right)^{-2}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}
. Thus, we now take the supremum over all compact operator with
\left\|{{\boldsymbol{b}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\leq 1
which leads to Inequality (
60
).

The proof of the estimate (
61
) is identical, one simply uses one commutator instead of two. This has the effect of only introducing a singular factor
(\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{m})^{-1}
in Equation (
62
) which then leads to
\tr(\left(\lambda-H\right)^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})
.

The claim of the proposition then follows by combining inequalities (
60
) and (
61
) with Lemma
3.1
and
3.2
with
\lambda\geq\hbar
.
∎

For the following proposition we introduce the shifted Hamiltonian

(64)

H_{\mu}:=H+\mu\geq 0\quad\text{ for }\quad\mu>\mu_{0}:=\left\|{V_{-}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\geq\left|{\inf\sigma(H)}\right|.

Proposition 3.5

(Second trace-class bounds)
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\mathds{1}_{H\leq 0}
with
H=-\hbar^{2}\Delta+V
in
d\geq 3
, and let
A
be a normal operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then, for all
\lambda\geq\hbar
and
\mu>\mu_{0}

h^{d}\tr\!\left(\left|{\left[{A,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right]}\right|\right)\leq\mathcal{C}_{\textnormal{Tr}}^{\prime}\left(\lambda\left\|{A\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}+\frac{1}{\lambda}\left\|{\left(\mathbf{1}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)\left[{\left[{A,H}\right],H_{\mu}^{1/2}}\right]{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\right).

Here
\mathcal{C}_{\textnormal{Tr}}^{\prime}=2\,\mathcal{C}_{1}+4\sqrt{1+\mu}\,\mathcal{C}_{2}
.

Proof.

Similarly as in the proof of Proposition
3.4
, we first write

\left\|{\left[{A,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right]}\right\|_{1}\leq 2\left\|{\left(\mathbf{1}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)A\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{1}\leq 2\left\|{\mathds{1}_{0<H<\lambda}\,A\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{1}+2\left\|{\mathds{1}_{H\geq\lambda}\,A\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{1}.

To bound the second term on the right-hand side, we take
{\boldsymbol{b}}
a compact operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. We now use a generalized version of (
59
) suitable for the square root functions. That is,

\mathbf{P}_{k}\,A\,\mathbf{P}_{j}=\frac{\mathbf{P}_{k}\left[{\left[{A,H}\right],\left(H+\mu\right)^{s}}\right]\mathbf{P}_{j}}{\left(\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{j}\right)\left(\left(\lambda_{k}+\mu\right)^{s}-\left(\lambda_{j}+\mu\right)^{s}\right)}\,.

Together with the identity
\frac{1}{\sqrt{b}-\sqrt{a}}\leq\frac{\sqrt{b}+\sqrt{a}}{b-a}
we obtain

\tr\!\left(\mathds{1}_{H\geq\lambda}\,A\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,{\boldsymbol{b}}\right)\leq\sum_{\lambda_{k}\geq\lambda}\sum_{\lambda_{j}\leq 0}\frac{2\sqrt{\lambda+\mu}}{(\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{j})^{2}}\tr\!\left(\mathbf{P}_{k}\left[{\left[{A,H}\right],H_{\mu}^{s}}\right]\mathbf{P}_{j}\,{\boldsymbol{b}}\right).

The proof then follows by the same steps as in the proof of Proposition
3.4
.
∎

3.5.
Proof of Theorems
1.1
and
1.3
– Application to semiclassical gradient estimates

Let us now apply the results of our general commutator estimates. More precisely, we will evaluate the operator
A
with the position operator
x
, the momentum operator
\boldsymbol{p}=-i\hbar\nabla
the shift operator
\tau_{z}
and obtain bounds for the quantum gradients of
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
.

With our notations for the quantum gradients (
12
) and the scaled Schatten norms (
13
), we recall that

\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}^{2}=\left(2\pi\right)^{2}h^{d-2}\tr\!\left(\left|{\left[{x,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right]}\right|^{2}\right),\qquad\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}^{2}=\left(2\pi\right)^{2}h^{d-2}\tr\!\left(\left|{\left[{\boldsymbol{p},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right]}\right|^{2}\right).

and

\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}^{2}=\left\|{\nabla_{\xi}f_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2},\qquad\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}^{2}=\left\|{\nabla_{x}f_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

We then have the following.

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
\hbar\in(0,1)
and
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\mathds{1}_{H\leq 0}
with
H=-\hbar^{2}\Delta+V
in
d\geq 3
. Then, it holds that

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathcal{C}_{\textnormal{HS}}}{\hbar}\left(\left\|{x\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}^{2}+4\left\|{\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathcal{C}_{\textnormal{HS}}}{\hbar}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}^{2}+\left\|{\nabla V{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\right)\,.

Additionally, for any
z\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}

\left\|{\mathsf{T}_{z}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\leq\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\min\!\left(\tfrac{\left|{z}\right|}{\sqrt{\hbar}},\sqrt{\left|{z}\right|},1\right)

where
\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\mathcal{C}_{\textnormal{HS}}\left(1+\left\|{\nabla V{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}^{2}+\left\|{x\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}^{2}+5\left\|{\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\right)+h^{d}\tr\!\left({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)
. If
\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
is bounded uniformly in
\hbar
, it implies in particular that there exists
C
independent of
\hbar
such that

\left\|{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{W}^{1,2}}\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{\hbar}}\quad\text{ and }\quad\left\|{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{B}^{1/2}_{2,\infty}}\leq C.

Let us now state our main results for the semiclassical gradients (
12
) in the trace-class norm. Again we recall that

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{1}}=2\pi\,h^{d-1}\tr{\left|{\left[{\boldsymbol{p},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right]}\right|}\quad\text{ and }\quad\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{1}}=2\pi\,h^{d-1}\tr{\left|{\left[{x,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right]}\right|}\,.

Proposition 3.7
.

Let
\hbar\in(0,1)
and
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\mathds{1}_{H\leq 0}
with
H=-\hbar^{2}\Delta+V
in
d\geq 3
. Then,

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{C}_{\textnormal{Tr}}\left(\left\|{x\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}+2\left\|{\nabla V{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\right)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{C}_{\textnormal{Tr}}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}+\left(1+\ln(1+\varepsilon_{0}/\hbar)\right)\left\|{\nabla V{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\right).

Additionally, for all
\mu>\mu_{0}=\left\|{V_{-}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}

\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{1}}\leq\mathcal{C}_{\textnormal{Tr}}^{\prime}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}+2\,\lVert\nabla^{2}V{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}+\tfrac{2}{\sqrt{\mu}}\lVert\nabla^{2}V\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\right).

Remark 3.5.1
.

It follows immediately from Identity (
66
) that

\left\|{\mathsf{T}_{z}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{1}}\leq\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{1}}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{1}}\right)\left|{z}\right|,

and it follows from the fact that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\varphi=h^{d}\tr\!\left(\left[{\nabla,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right]\varphi\right)
that

(65)

\left\|{\nabla\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{L^{1}}\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{1}}.

Proof of Proposition
3.6
.

Taking
A=\boldsymbol{p}
or
A=x
in Proposition
3.3
gives directly the two first inequalities. Next, consider
z=(y,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
so that, by
[
38
, Formula (28)]
, we have

(66)

\mathsf{T}_{z}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=z\cdot\int_{0}^{1}\mathsf{T}_{\theta z}\boldsymbol{\nabla}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta

where
\boldsymbol{\nabla}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=(\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}},\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})
. Thus, we find in Schatten norm

\left\|{\mathsf{T}_{z}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq\mathcal{C}_{\textnormal{HS}}\,\frac{\left|{z}\right|^{2}}{\hbar}\left(5\left\|{\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}^{2}+\left\|{x\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}^{2}+\left\|{\nabla V{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\right).

This is sufficient to analyze
\left|{z}\right|\leq\hbar
. Consider now
\hbar\leq\left|{z}\right|\leq 1
. Since
\mathsf{T}_{z}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\tau_{z}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\tau_{z}^{*}
with
\tau_{z}
unitary, it follows that

\left\|{\mathsf{T}_{z}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}=\left\|{\tau_{z}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\tau_{z}^{*}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\tau_{z}\,\tau_{z}^{*}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}=\left\|{\left[{\tau_{z},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right]}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}.

We now take
\lambda=\left|{z}\right|\leq 1
in Inequality (
55
) with
A=\tau_{z}
. Thanks to Lemma
3.1
and
3.2
we then get

\left\|{\mathsf{T}_{z}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq\mathcal{C}_{\textnormal{HS}}\left(\left|{z}\right|\left\|{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}^{2}+\frac{1}{\left|{z}\right|}\left\|{\left(\mathsf{T}_{z}H-H\right){\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\right).

Using Formula (
66
) for
H
gives

\left\|{\left(\mathsf{T}_{z}H-H\right){\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\leq\left|{z}\right|^{2}\left(2\left\|{\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}^{2}+\left\|{\nabla V{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\right).

This yields

\left\|{\mathsf{T}_{z}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq\mathcal{C}_{\textnormal{HS}}\left|{z}\right|\left(\left\|{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}^{2}+2\left\|{\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}^{2}+\left\|{\nabla V{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\right).

Finally, for
\left|{z}\right|\geq 1
, it is sufficient to use the triangle inequality for Schatten norms and the fact that
\mathsf{T}_{z}
is an isometry to get

\left\|{\mathsf{T}_{z}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq 2\left\|{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}^{2}=2\,h^{d}\tr\!\left({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right).

This finishes the proof after we collect all the constants in
\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
.
∎

We need the following lemma to evaluate the commutator with the fractional operator in the trace norm. In practice, we take
A=\nabla
, which yields
\left[{\nabla,H}\right]=\nabla V
. Thus it suffices to compute the commutator with the fractional operator in the case of multiplication operators.

Lemma 3.8

(Fractional commutator)
.

Let
d\geq 1
,
\mu>\mu_{0}=\left\|{V_{-}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}
and
\phi\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
seen as an operator of multiplication. Then

\left\|{\left(\mathbf{1}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)\left[{\left(H+\mu\right)^{1/2},\phi}\right]{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\leq 2\,\hbar\left(\left\|{\nabla\phi\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}+\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\mu}}\left\|{\nabla\phi\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\right).

Proof.

Recall
H_{\mu}=H+\mu\geq-\left\|{V_{-}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}+\mu>0
. To compute the fractional commutator we use the formula

H_{\mu}^{1/2}=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{H_{\mu}}{H_{\mu}+t}\,\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t^{1/2}}

which gives

\displaystyle\pi\left[{H_{\mu}^{1/2},\phi}\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{H_{\mu}+t}\left[{\phi,H}\right]\frac{H_{\mu}}{H_{\mu}+t}\,\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t^{1/2}}+\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{H_{\mu}+t}\left[{\phi,H}\right]\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t^{1/2}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{H_{\mu}+t}\left[{\phi,H}\right]\frac{H_{\mu}}{H_{\mu}+t}\,\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t^{1/2}}+\pi\,H_{\mu}^{1/2}\left[{\phi,H}\right].

Since
\left[{\phi,H}\right]=i\hbar\left(\boldsymbol{p}\cdot\nabla\phi+\nabla\phi\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\right)
where
\nabla\phi
is the operator of multiplication by
\nabla\phi
, and
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
commutes with
H_{\mu}
, it yields

I:=\frac{\pi}{\hbar}\left\|{\left(\mathbf{1}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)\left[{H_{\mu}^{1/2},\phi}\right]{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\leq\pi\left\|{\left(\mathbf{1}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)H_{\mu}^{1/2}\left(\boldsymbol{p}\cdot\nabla\phi+\nabla\phi\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\right){\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\\
+\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\left\|{\frac{\mathds{1}_{H_{\mu}\geq\mu}}{H_{\mu}+t}\,\nabla\phi\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\frac{H_{\mu}}{H_{\mu}+t}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}+\left\|{\frac{\mathds{1}_{H_{\mu}\geq\mu}}{H_{\mu}+t}\,\boldsymbol{p}\cdot\nabla\phi\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\frac{H_{\mu}}{H_{\mu}+t}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\right)\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t^{1/2}}\,.

Observing that
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\mathds{1}_{0\leq H_{\mu}\leq\mu}
,
\mathbf{1}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\mathds{1}_{H_{\mu}\geq\mu}
and
u\mapsto u/(u+t)
is increasing on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, it follows that

I\leq\pi\left(\left\|{H_{\mu}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{p}\cdot\nabla\phi\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}+\mu^{-1/2}\left\|{\nabla\phi\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\right)\\
+\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{1}{\mu+t}\left\|{\nabla\phi\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}+\frac{1}{(\mu+t)^{1/2}}\left\|{H_{\mu}^{-1/2}\,\boldsymbol{p}\cdot\nabla\phi\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\right)\frac{\mu}{\mu+t}\,\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t^{1/2}}\,.

Notice that since
\mu\geq\left\|{V_{-}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}
, it follows that

\left|{\boldsymbol{p}\,H_{\mu}^{-1/2}}\right|^{2}=H_{\mu}^{-1/2}\left|{\boldsymbol{p}}\right|^{2}H_{\mu}^{-1/2}=H_{\mu}^{-1/2}\left(H_{\mu}-V-\mu\right)H_{\mu}^{-1/2}\leq 1\,.

Therefore, it gives

I\leq\pi\left(\left\|{\nabla\phi\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}+\mu^{-1/2}\left\|{\nabla\phi\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\right)\\
+\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{1}{\mu+t}\left\|{\nabla\phi\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}+\frac{1}{(\mu+t)^{1/2}}\left\|{\nabla\phi\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\right)\frac{\mu}{\mu+t}\,\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t^{1/2}}\,.

The result follows by computing the integrals in
t
and using
1+\frac{2}{\pi},\frac{3}{2}\leq 2
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.7
.

Taking
A=\boldsymbol{p}
or
A=x
in the first estimate in Proposition
3.4
gives directly the two first inequalities. Now for
\mu>\mu_{0}
we use the fractional commutator inequalities for
A=\boldsymbol{p}
. In this case
\left[{\left[{\boldsymbol{p},H}\right],H_{\mu}^{1/2}}\right]=-i\hbar\left[{\nabla V,H_{\mu}^{1/2}}\right]
. It follows from Lemma
3.8
that

\left\|{\left(\mathbf{1}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)\left[{\left[{\boldsymbol{p},H}\right],H_{\mu}^{1/2}}\right]{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\leq 2\,\hbar^{2}\left(\left\|{\nabla^{2}V{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}+\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\mu}}\left\|{\nabla^{2}V\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\right).

It then suffices to apply Proposition
3.5
, which finishes the proof.
∎

We are now ready to give a proof of our first main result, Theorem
1.1
.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

In the proof, we make use of the estimates coming from propositions
3.6
and
3.7
. Observe that they depend only upon the constants
\mathcal{C}_{\textnormal{HS}}
,
\mathcal{C}_{\textnormal{Tr}}
,
\mathcal{C}_{\textnormal{Tr}}^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
, which are manifestly independent of
\hbar
. For the proof, let us set
\mathcal{C}_{*}
as
5
times the maximum of these constants. Before we turn to the proof of the commutator estimates, let us give some preliminary estimates on the various moments that appear in the bounds. Namely, thanks to Inequality (
44
), Inequality (
34
), Inequality (
42
) and Lemma
2.2
we have for all
0<\beta\leq 1/4\hbar

(67)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2}\left\|{V_{-}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}^{1/2}

(68)

\displaystyle\left\|{e^{\beta\,d_{1}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq e^{\beta}+C_{d}\left\|{V_{-}}\right\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}}^{p^{\prime}/2}

(69)

\displaystyle\left\|{e^{\beta d_{1}}\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2}\,e^{\beta}\left\|{V_{-}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}+\sqrt{e}\,C_{d}\left\|{V_{-}}\right\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}}^{p^{\prime}/2}

where
p^{\prime}=1+\frac{d}{2}
,
C_{d}=2/\sqrt{3}\left(2d/(\pi e)\right)^{d/2}C_{\textnormal{LT}}^{1/2}
. Let us consider now
R>0
so that
\Omega_{1}=\{V\leq 1\}\subset B_{R}
. In particular,
d_{1}\geq\left|{x}\right|-R
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We then obtain

(70)

\displaystyle\left\|{x\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq e^{R}\left\|{\left|{x}\right|e^{-\beta\left|{x}\right|}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\left(e^{\beta}+C_{d}\left\|{V_{-}}\right\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}}^{p^{\prime}/2}\right)

(71)

\displaystyle\left\|{\nabla V{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq e^{R}\left\|{\nabla Ve^{-\beta\left|{x}\right|}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\left(e^{\beta}+C_{d}\left\|{V_{-}}\right\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}}^{p^{\prime}/2}\right)

(72)

\displaystyle\left\|{\nabla^{2}V{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq e^{R}\left\|{\nabla^{2}Ve^{-\beta\left|{x}\right|}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\left(e^{\beta}+C_{d}\left\|{V_{-}}\right\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}}^{p^{\prime}/2}\right)

(73)

\displaystyle\left\|{\nabla^{2}V\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq e^{R}\left\|{\nabla^{2}Ve^{-\beta\left|{x}\right|}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\left(\sqrt{2}\,e^{\beta}\left\|{V_{-}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}+\sqrt{e}\,C_{d}\left\|{V_{-}}\right\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}}^{p^{\prime}/2}\right).

Let us turn to the proof of the commutator estimates for
V
satisfying
(
H1
)
with parameter
\beta\geq 0
. Note that if
\beta\,\hbar>1/4
, then we have

\left\|{\left[{x,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right]}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq 4\left\|{x\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\left\|{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{1}}\leq 16\,\beta\,\hbar\,\mathsf{L}_{0,d}\left\|{x\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}

where we used
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{2}={\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
and the CLR inequality (
25
). Note now that
\left\|{x\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}
is uniformly bounded in
\hbar\leq 1
thanks to Inequality (
70
) with parameter
0<\beta_{1}\leq 1/4
. An analogous argument is also true for the commutator with
\boldsymbol{p}
.

Thus, let us assume
0\leq\beta\,\hbar\leq 1/4
. First, from Proposition
3.6
we get

\displaystyle\left\|{\left[{x,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right]}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{C}_{*}^{1/2}\hbar^{1/2}\left(\left\|{x\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\right)

\displaystyle\left\|{\left[{\boldsymbol{p},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right]}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{C}_{*}^{1/2}\hbar^{1/2}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}+\left\|{\nabla V{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\right).

For
V
satisfying (
H1
), the right-hand side becomes bounded uniformly in
\hbar
thanks to inequalities (
67
), (
70
) and (
71
). This proves the case
p=2
. In the case
p=\infty
, it suffices to write the commutator as a difference and use the triangle inequality for the operator norm to get

\left\|{\left[{x,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right]}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\leq 2\left\|{x\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\quad\text{ and }\quad\left\|{\left[{\boldsymbol{p},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right]}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\leq 2\left\|{\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}.

Again, in each case, the right-hand side becomes bounded uniformly in
\hbar
thanks to inequalities (
67
) and (
70
). The case
p\in(2,\infty)
now follows by interpolation of the case
p=2
and
p=\infty
, that is, by Hölder’s inequality for Schatten norms,

\left\|{\left[{x,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right]}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{p}}\leq\left\|{\left[{x,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right]}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}^{2/p}\left\|{\left[{x,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right]}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}^{1-2/p}\leq C\,\hbar^{1/p}.

and similarly for
\left[{\boldsymbol{p},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right]
.

As for the trace-class estimates, we apply Proposition
3.7
to find

\displaystyle h^{d}\tr\!\left(\left|{\left[{x,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right]}\right|\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{C}_{*}\,\hbar\left(\left\|{x\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}+\left\|{\nabla V{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\right)

\displaystyle h^{d}\tr\!\left(\left|{\left[{\boldsymbol{p},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right]}\right|\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{C}_{*}\,\hbar\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}+\ln(1+\varepsilon_{0}\,\hbar^{-1})\left\|{\nabla V{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\right).

Thanks to inequalities (
67
), (
70
) and (
71
), this proves the claim for
p=1
. The case
p\in(1,2)
now follows by interpolation of the cases
p=1
and
p=2
.

Finally, assume
e^{-\beta\left|{x}\right|}\,\nabla^{2}V\in L^{\infty}
uniformly in
\hbar
. Then, Proposition
3.7
for
\mu=\left\|{V_{-}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}+1
implies

h^{d}\tr\!\left(\left|{\left[{\boldsymbol{p},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right]}\right|\right)\leq\mathcal{C}_{*}\,\hbar\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}+\lVert\nabla^{2}V{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}+\lVert\nabla^{2}V\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\right).

Thanks to inequalities (
67
), (
72
) and (
73
), this proves the claim for
p=1
and the case
p\in(1,2)
follows again by interpolation.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.3
.

For
p=2
the claim follows from Proposition
3.6
. The constant
\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
is estimated similarly as in the proof of Theorem
1.1
. For
p=1
, the claim follows from the trace-class commutator estimates in Theorem
1.1
and the fact that for any
z\in\mathbb{R}^{6}
, by Formula (
66
),

\left\|{\mathsf{T}_{z}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{1}}\leq\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{1}}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{1}}\right)\left|{z}\right|.

For
p=\infty
, notice that on the one hand, since
0\leq{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\leq 1
and
0\leq\mathsf{T}_{z}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\leq 1
, it follows that
\left\|{\mathsf{T}_{z}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\leq 1
. On the other hand, Formula (
66
) gives

\left\|{\mathsf{T}_{z}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\leq\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\right)\left|{z}\right|

and
\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}
and
\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}
are bounded uniformly in
\hbar
similarly as in the proof of Theorem
1.1
. For every other
p\in[1,\infty]
we can use an interpolation argument, i.e. Hölder’s inequality for Schatten norms.
∎

4.
Quantitative local Weyl’s law

The main goal of this section is to prove Theorem
1.4
. To this end, we shall obtain quantitative versions of the local Weyl law and the Weyl law in the phase space. The proof of the theorem then follows from propositions
4.1
and
4.2
and will be given at the end of this section. In what follows,
\beta\geq 0
will be a fixed non-negative parameter.

Let us introduce some notations in order to keep track of the constants. We recall
\Omega_{a}
and
\Omega_{a,b}

where introduced in (
30
). We denote by
\mathcal{M}
the space of measures on
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
with finite total variation, with norm
\left\|{F}\right\|_{\mathcal{M}}
. We let
g_{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon^{-d/2}\,e^{-\pi\left|{x}\right|^{2}/\varepsilon}
be a Gaussian at scale
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Next, we introduce the following quantities for any classical function
f
, and operator
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
. First,

(74)

\mathsf{M}_{f,{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}=\frac{d}{8\pi}\left(M_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}+M_{f}\right),\qquad M_{f}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}f(x,\xi)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi\,,\qquad M_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}=h^{d}\tr\!\left({\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right).

Secondly, for
q\in\{1,2\}

(75)

\displaystyle\mathsf{L}_{f,{\boldsymbol{\rho}},1}=\frac{2\sqrt{p^{\prime}}}{p}\omega_{d}^{1/d}C_{d,\beta}\left(\left\|{e^{\beta\left|{x}\right|}\varrho_{f}}\right\|_{L^{1}}^{\frac{d-2}{d}}+\left\|{e^{\beta\left|{x}\right|}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\right\|_{L^{1}}^{\frac{d-2}{d}}\right)

(76)

\displaystyle\mathsf{L}_{f,{\boldsymbol{\rho}},2}=\frac{2\sqrt{p^{\prime}}}{p}\omega_{d}^{1/d}\left(\left\|{\varrho_{f}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{d-2}{d}}+\left\|{\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{d-2}{d}}\right)

with
p=1+\frac{2}{d}
and
C_{d,\beta}=\left\|{e^{-\frac{(d-2)\beta}{2d}\left|{x}\right|}}\right\|_{L^{d}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\left\|{e^{\beta\left|{x}\right|}g_{1}}\right\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
. Finally,

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{f,{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{d}{\pi}\min\!\left(\left\|{\nabla V_{-}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\left\|{\nabla(\varrho_{f}+\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\rho}})}\right\|_{L^{1}},\left\|{V}\right\|_{W^{2,1}(\Omega_{1})}\left\|{\varrho_{f}+\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|{x}\right|^{2}e^{-\pi\left|{x}\right|^{2}+\beta\left|{x}\right|}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\left\|{e^{-\beta\left|{x}\right|}\nabla V_{+}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\left\|{e^{+\beta\left|{x}\right|}\nabla\varrho_{f}}\right\|_{L^{1}}.

We now state our result regarding convergence of the densities.

Proposition 4.1

(Convergence of densities)
.

Let
\hbar\in(0,1)
,
f=\mathds{1}_{\left|{\xi}\right|^{2}+V\leq 0}
and
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\mathds{1}_{H\leq 0}
where
H=-\hbar^{2}\Delta+V
, in
d\geq 3
. Denote
\mathsf{M}:=\mathsf{M}_{f,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}
,
\mathsf{L}_{q}:=\mathsf{L}_{f,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}},q}
and
\mathsf{D}:=\mathsf{D}_{f,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}
. Then, there exists a constant
C_{d}>0
such that

\displaystyle\left\|{\varrho_{f}-\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{d}\,h^{1/3}(1+\left\|{\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\dot{B}^{1/2}_{2,\infty}})^{2/3}\left(1+\mathsf{L}_{2}\,\mathsf{M}^{1/2}+\mathsf{L}_{2}\,\mathsf{D}^{1/2}h^{1/3}\right)

\displaystyle\left\|{\varrho_{f}-\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{d}\,h^{1/2}\left(1+\left\|{\nabla\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{L^{1}}^{1/2}+\mathsf{D}^{1/2}\right)\left(1+\mathsf{L}_{1}+\mathsf{M}^{1/2}\right).

For the next result, we introduce the Husimi transform of the density operator
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
as follows

m_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}:=g_{h/2}\otimes g_{h/2}*f_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,.

For notational convenience, we introduce the following two constants, depending on
V
and
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
only through quantities that are uniformly bounded in
\hbar
:

(77)

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{1}

\displaystyle=d\,\omega_{d}^{\frac{2}{d}}\left|{\Omega_{1,1}}\right|^{\frac{2}{d}}\,M_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{1-\frac{2}{d}}+\left|{\Omega_{1,1}}\right|2^{\frac{d}{2}}

(78)

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{2}

\displaystyle=\left(2\left(\frac{d+2}{2e\pi}\right)^{1+\frac{d}{2}}+\frac{8}{3\pi}\right)M_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\left(1+\left\|{V_{-}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\right).

Proposition 4.2

(Convergence of states)
.

With the same notations as in Proposition
4.1

\left\|{f-m_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}\leq h^{1/2}\left(\left(\mathsf{L}_{1}+\mathsf{C}_{1}\right)\left(\mathsf{M}+\mathsf{D}\right)+\mathsf{C}_{2}\right).

Additionally, there exists
C_{d}>0
such that

\displaystyle\left\|{f-f_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{d}\,h^{1/4}\left\|{f}\right\|_{\dot{B}_{2,\infty}^{1/2}}+\left\|{f-m_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\left\|{{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{f}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{d}\,h^{1/2}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nabla}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{1}}+\left\|{\nabla f}\right\|_{\mathcal{M}}\right)+\left\|{f-m_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}.

We now follow the classic strategy of using coherent states together with the above variational principle to first get the following quantitative version of the Weyl law. To optimize the rate of convergence, we use anisotropic coherent states. Let
g_{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon^{-d/2}\,e^{-\pi\left|{x}\right|^{2}/\varepsilon}
. Then we can consider the function of the phase space

G_{\varepsilon}(x,\xi):=g_{\varepsilon}(x)\,g_{\frac{h^{2}}{4\varepsilon}}(\xi)\,.

Computing the kernel of its Weyl quantization gives

{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{G_{\varepsilon}}(x,y)=h^{-d}\,g_{\varepsilon}(\tfrac{x+y}{2})\,\widehat{g}_{\frac{h^{2}}{4\varepsilon}}(\tfrac{x-y}{h})=h^{-d}\varepsilon^{-d/2}\,e^{-\pi\,\frac{\left|{x}\right|^{2}+\left|{y}\right|^{2}}{2\varepsilon}}

that is,
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{G_{\varepsilon}}=h^{-d}\ket{\psi_{\varepsilon}}\bra{\psi_{\varepsilon}}
with
\psi_{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon^{-d/4}\,e^{-\pi\left|{x}\right|^{2}/(2\varepsilon)}
. In particular,
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{G_{\varepsilon}}\geq 0
.

To keep our notations close to the classical case, we will write the coherent states transform (or Toeplitz operator, or Wick quantization) induced by these coherent states using in the form of a semiclassical convolution, as introduced already for instance in
[
57
,
38
]
. This semiclassical convolution is defined for any operator
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}
and function
f\in L^{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
by

f\star{\boldsymbol{\rho}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}g(z)\,\mathsf{T}_{z}{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z

where
\mathsf{T}_{z}
is the operator of translation in the phase space
\mathsf{T}_{z}{\boldsymbol{\rho}}:=\tau_{z}\,{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\,\tau_{-z}
with
\tau_{z}=e^{-i\,z_{0}^{\perp}\cdot\mathbf{z}/\hbar}
. It is a positive operator whenever
g\geq 0
and
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\geq 0
and it verifies the analogue of Young’s inequalities (see
[
57
]
):
\left\|{g\star{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{p}}\leq\left\|{g}\right\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}\left\|{{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{r}}
if
1+\frac{1}{p}=\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{r}
. In particular, we denote by

\,\tilde{\!{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}:=G_{\varepsilon}\star{\boldsymbol{\rho}}

and the Toeplitz operator of a function
f
induced by these coherent states is nothing but
\,\tilde{\!{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}_{f}=G_{\varepsilon}\star{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{f}=f\star{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{G_{\varepsilon}}
. In particular, if
0\leq f\leq 1
, then

0\leq\,\tilde{\!{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}_{f}\leq 1
in the sense of operators.
We also denote by
\tilde{f}
the function of the phase space
\tilde{f}:=G_{\varepsilon}*f
, so that
\,\tilde{\!{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}_{f}={\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{\tilde{f}}
. Notice also that
\tilde{f}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
is then the Husimi function associated to
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
induced by this anisotropic family of coherent states.

Let us first recall the following result sometimes referred to as the variational principle or the generalized min-max principle (see e.g.
[
42
,
24
]
) and additionally give a quantitative version of it.

Lemma 4.3

(Quantitative Variational Principle)
.

Let
H=-\hbar^{2}\Delta+V
and
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\mathds{1}_{H\leq 0}
. Then, for any operator
0\leq{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\leq 1
there holds

(79)

\tr\!\left(H\left({\boldsymbol{\rho}}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)\right)=\tr\!\left(\left|{H}\right|\left|{{\boldsymbol{\rho}}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right|^{2}\right)+\tr\!\left(\left|{H}\right|{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\left(\mathbf{1}-{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right)\right).

Similarly, if
\mathcal{H}(x,\xi)=\left|{\xi}\right|^{2}+V(x)
and
f=\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{H}\leq 0}
. Then, for any function
0\leq g\leq 1

(80)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\mathcal{H}\left(g-f\right)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\left|{\mathcal{H}}\right|\left|{f-g}\right|.

Remark 4.0.1
.

The quantum version is reminiscent of the fact that in the classical version, we may write the right-hand side as follows

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\mathcal{H}\left(g-f\right)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\left|{\mathcal{H}}\right|\left|{f-g}\right|^{2}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\left|{\mathcal{H}}\right|g\left(1-g\right).

Proof.

Since
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{2}={\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\tr\!\left(H\left({\boldsymbol{\rho}}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\tr\!\left(H\left(\mathbf{1}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)\left({\boldsymbol{\rho}}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)\right)+\tr\!\left(H\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\left({\boldsymbol{\rho}}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\tr\!\left(\left|{H}\right|\left(\mathbf{1}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right){\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right)+\tr\!\left(\left|{H}\right|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\left(\mathbf{1}-{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right)\right)

which can also be written as

\tr\!\left(H\left({\boldsymbol{\rho}}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)\right)=\tr\!\left(\left|{H}\right|\left({\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{2}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,{\boldsymbol{\rho}}-{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}+{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{2}\right)\right)+\tr\!\left(\left|{H}\right|\left({\boldsymbol{\rho}}-{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{2}\right)\right)

which gives Identity (
79
). The classical case is treated analogously by splitting the integral into the regions where
f=0
and
f=1
.
∎

Let us introduce here the linear version of the functional
\mathscr{E}
. That is,

\mathcal{E}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}:=\tr\!\left(\left(-\hbar^{2}\Delta+V\right){\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right),\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathcal{E}_{f}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\left(\left|{\xi}\right|^{2}+V(x)\right)f(x,\xi)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi\,.

Thanks to the properties of the Wigner transform, we obtain that for any
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
and
f

(81)

\mathcal{E}_{{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{f}}=\mathcal{E}_{f}\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathcal{E}_{f_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}=\mathcal{E}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\,.

We use the following notation for a classical function
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

\mathcal{H}_{g}(x,\xi):=\left|{\xi}\right|^{2}-c_{d}\,\varrho_{g}(x)^{\frac{2}{d}}\quad\text{ with }\quad c_{d}=\omega_{d}^{-\,\frac{2}{d}}\,.

We also introduce the following notations in order to keep track of the classical and quantum errors

(82)

\displaystyle Q(g)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\left|{\mathcal{H}_{g}}\right|\left|{\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{g}\leq 0}-g}\right|

(83)

\displaystyle\widetilde{Q}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}({\boldsymbol{\rho}})

\displaystyle:=h^{d}\tr\!\left(\left|{H}\right|\left|{{\boldsymbol{\rho}}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right|^{2}+\left|{H}\right|({\boldsymbol{\rho}}-{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{2})\right)

for any classical function
0\leq g\leq 1
, and any operator
0\leq{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\leq\mathbf{1}
.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
\mathcal{H}:=\left|{\xi}\right|^{2}+V(x)
and
f:=\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{H}\leq 0}
. Then, for any function
0\leq g\leq 1

(84)

\mathcal{E}_{g}-\mathcal{E}_{f}=Q(g)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}V_{+}\,\varrho_{g}+\frac{c_{d}}{p}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{g}^{p}-\varrho_{f}^{p}-p\,\varrho_{f}^{p-1}\left(\varrho_{g}-\varrho_{f}\right)

with
p=1+\frac{2}{d}
and
c_{d}=\omega_{d}^{-\,2/d}
. In particular, the content of the third integral verifies

(85)

\varrho_{g}^{p}-\varrho_{f}^{p}-p\,\varrho_{f}^{p-1}\left(\varrho_{g}-\varrho_{f}\right)\geq\left(p-1\right)\left|{\varrho_{g}^{p/2}-\varrho_{f}^{p/2}}\right|^{2}.

Proof.

Notice that, since
\varrho_{f}=\omega_{d}\,V_{-}^{d/2}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\left(\left|{\xi}\right|^{2}+V\right)\left(g-f\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\left|{\xi}\right|^{2}\left(g-f\right)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}V\left(\varrho_{g}-\varrho_{f}\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\left|{\xi}\right|^{2}\left(g-f\right)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}V_{+}\,\varrho_{g}-c_{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{f}^{\frac{2}{d}}\left(\varrho_{g}-\varrho_{f}\right).

Therefore, using the fact that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}f\left|{\xi}\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi=\frac{c_{d}}{p}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{f}^{p}
, it gives

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\left(\left|{\xi}\right|^{2}+V\right)\left(g-f\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}V_{+}\,\varrho_{g}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\mathcal{H}_{g}\,g\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi\\
+\frac{c_{d}}{p}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}p\,\varrho_{g}^{p}-\varrho_{f}^{p}-p\,\varrho_{f}^{p-1}\left(\varrho_{g}-\varrho_{f}\right).

Now using the classical quantitative bathtub principle formula (
80
) gives

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\mathcal{H}_{g}\left(g-\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{g}\leq 0}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\left|{\mathcal{H}_{g}}\right|\left|{\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{g}\leq 0}-g}\right|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi

and on the other side,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\mathcal{H}_{g}\,\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{g}\leq 0}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\left(\left|{\xi}\right|^{2}-c_{d}\,\varrho_{g}^{2/d}\right)_{-}=-\frac{c_{d}}{p^{\prime}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{g}^{p}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y

where
p^{\prime}
is the dual exponent of
p
. This proves Identity (
84
). To get Inequality (
85
), one can use Young’s inequality for the product to get that for any
c>0
,
c\leq\left(\frac{2}{p}-1\right)c^{p}+\frac{2}{p^{\prime}}\,c^{p/2}
which can be written
\left(p-1\right)\left|{c^{p/2}-1}\right|^{2}\leq c^{p}+1-p\left(c-1\right)
, and implies that for any
a,b\geq 0

\left(p-1\right)\left|{b^{p/2}-a^{p/2}}\right|^{2}\leq b^{p}-a^{p}-p\,a^{p-1}\left(b-a\right),

which implies the result by taking
a=\varrho_{f}
and
b=\varrho_{g}
.
∎

Corollary 4.5

(Control via energies)
.

Let
H=-\hbar^{2}\Delta+V
,
\mathcal{H}=\left|{\xi}\right|^{2}+V(x)
,
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\mathds{1}_{H\leq 0}
,
f=\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{H}\leq 0}
.
Then

Q(f_{\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}})+\widetilde{Q}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}({\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{\tilde{f}})+\frac{c_{d}}{p^{\prime}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|{\varrho_{\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}}^{p/2}-\varrho_{f}^{p/2}}\right|^{2}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}V_{+}\,\varrho_{\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}}\leq\mathcal{E}_{\tilde{f}}-\mathcal{E}_{f}+\mathcal{E}_{\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}}-\mathcal{E}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,.

Here
p=1+\frac{2}{d}
,
c_{d}=\omega_{d}^{-2/d}
and
Q
and
\widetilde{Q}
are defined in equations (
82
) and (
83
).

Proof.

We employ Equation (
79
) with
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}={\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{\tilde{f}}
, Lemma
4.4
with
g=\tilde{f}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
, the relations (
81
), and the second inequality in Lemma
4.4
.
∎

The previous corollary establishes that the energy differences control various quantities of interest. In our next lemma, we establish estimates that will be helpful in the control of the energies. Recall
\mathsf{M}
was introduced in Proposition
4.1
.

Lemma 4.6

(Energy estimates)
.

Take the same notations as in Corollary
4.5
. Then if
V_{-}\in W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\tilde{f}}-\mathcal{E}_{f}+\mathcal{E}_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}-\mathcal{E}_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\leq\frac{h^{2}}{\varepsilon}\mathsf{M}+\frac{d\varepsilon}{4\pi}\left\|{\nabla V_{-}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\left\|{\nabla(\varrho_{f}+\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})}\right\|_{L^{1}}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(g_{\varepsilon}*V_{+}\right)\left(\varrho_{f}+\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right).

On the other hand, if
V\in W^{2,1}(\Omega)
where
\Omega
is an open set containing
\{V\leq 0\}
, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\tilde{f}}-\mathcal{E}_{f}+\mathcal{E}_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}-\mathcal{E}_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\leq\frac{h^{2}}{\varepsilon}\mathsf{M}+\frac{d\,\varepsilon}{\pi}\left\|{V}\right\|_{W^{2,1}(\Omega)}\left\|{\varrho_{f}+\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(g_{\varepsilon}*V_{+}\right)\left(\varrho_{f}+\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right).

Additionally, we also have the estimate for the positive part

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(g_{\varepsilon}*V_{+}\right)\varrho_{f}

\displaystyle\leq C_{d,\beta}\,\varepsilon\left\|{e^{-\beta\left|{x}\right|}\nabla V_{+}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\left\|{e^{+\beta\left|{x}\right|}\nabla\varrho_{f}}\right\|_{L^{1}}

where
C_{d,\beta}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{\beta\left|{x}\right|}\left|{x}\right|^{2}g_{1}(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
.

Proof.

In general, since
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\left|{\xi}\right|^{2}G_{\varepsilon}(z)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z=\frac{d\,h^{2}}{8\pi\,\varepsilon}
,
G_{\varepsilon}
is even and
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}f=M_{f}
, it follows that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\left|{\xi}\right|^{2}\tilde{f}(z)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\left|{\xi}\right|^{2}f(z)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z+\frac{d\,h^{2}}{8\pi\,\varepsilon}\,M_{f}

which yields

\mathcal{E}_{\tilde{f}}=\mathcal{E}_{f}+\frac{d\,h^{2}}{8\pi\,\varepsilon}\,M_{f}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(g_{\varepsilon}*V-V\right)\varrho_{f}\,.

An analogous proof in the case of quantum densities yields

\mathcal{E}_{\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}}=\mathcal{E}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}+\frac{d\,h^{2}}{8\pi\,\varepsilon}M_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(g_{\varepsilon}*V-V\right)\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,.

In what follows, we let
\rho=\varrho_{r}+\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
. First, we decompose
V=V_{+}-V_{-}
and for the part containing
V_{-}
, we use a symmetrization argument to write

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(g_{\varepsilon}*V_{-}-V_{-}\right)\rho=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\left(V_{-}(x)-V_{-}(y)\right)g_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\left(\rho(x)-\rho(y)\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\,.

First, assume
V_{-}\in W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then, a standard Taylor argument shows that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(g_{\varepsilon}*V_{-}-V_{-}\right)\rho\leq\frac{d\,\varepsilon}{4\pi}\left\|{\nabla V_{-}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\left\|{\nabla\rho}\right\|_{L^{1}}.

Secondly, assume
V_{-}\in W^{2,1}(\Omega)
. We let
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d},[0,1])
be so that
\chi=1
on
\Omega
and
\chi=0
on the complement of
\Omega_{1}=\{x:\dist(x,\Omega)<1\}
. Here,
\chi
can be chosen so that
\left\|{\nabla\chi}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq 2
and
\left\|{\nabla^{2}\chi}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq 2
. Write
V=\chi\,V+\left(1-\chi\right)V
so that

g*V-V=g*\left(\chi\,V\right)-\chi\,V+g*\left(\left(1-\chi\right)V\right)-\left(1-\chi\right)V\,.

Observe that
1-\chi
is supported on
\{V\geq 0\}
. Thus for any
\rho\geq 0

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(g*V-V\right)\rho\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(g*\left(\chi\,V\right)-\chi\,V\right)\rho+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(g*V_{+}\right)\rho\,.

A second order Taylor expansion for
V_{\chi}:=\chi\,V
and the fact that
g_{\varepsilon}
is even imply

\displaystyle V_{\chi}-g_{\varepsilon}*V_{\chi}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(V_{\chi}(x)-V_{\chi}(x-y)\right)g_{\varepsilon}(y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}g_{\varepsilon}(y)\,y\otimes y:\int_{0}^{1}\left(1-\theta\right)\nabla^{2}V_{\chi}(x-\theta\,y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y

where
y\otimes y:\nabla^{2}V
is the double contraction of tensors, that is
y\otimes y:\nabla^{2}V=y\cdot\nabla^{2}V\cdot y
. The desired Inequality then follows from Hölder’s inequality and the product rule

\left\|{\nabla^{2}V_{\chi}}\right\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\left\|{\nabla^{2}V}\right\|_{L^{1}(\Omega_{1})}+4\left\|{\nabla V}\right\|_{L^{1}(\Omega_{1})}+2\left\|{V}\right\|_{L^{1}(\Omega_{1})}\leq 4\left\|{V}\right\|_{W^{2,1}(\Omega_{1})}

which finishes the proof in this case.

Finally, we look at the
V_{+}
part for
\varrho_{f}=\omega_{d}\,V_{-}^{d/2}
. Let us symmetrize the integrals, change variables
x\mapsto x+y
, and do a Taylor expansion to see

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}g_{\varepsilon}*V_{+}\varrho_{f}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\left(V_{+}(y+x)-V_{+}(y)\right)g_{\varepsilon}(x)\left(\varrho_{f}(y+x)-\varrho_{f}(y)\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}x\cdot\nabla V_{+}\left(y+t_{1}x\right)g_{\varepsilon}(x)\,x\cdot\nabla\varrho_{f}\left(y+t_{2}x\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{2}\,.

Next, we change
y\mapsto y-t_{2}\,x
and use
\left|{t_{1}-t_{2}}\right|\leq 1
to find

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}g_{\varepsilon}*V_{+}\varrho_{f}\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|{x}\right|^{2}e^{\beta\left|{x}\right|}g_{\varepsilon}(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\left\|{e^{-\beta\left|{x}\right|}\,\nabla V_{+}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\left\|{e^{\beta\left|{y}\right|}\varrho_{f}}\right\|_{L^{1}}\,.

Thanks to
\varepsilon\leq 1
we have the bound
\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{\beta\left|{x}\right|}\left|{x}\right|^{2}g_{\varepsilon}(x)\leq C_{d,\beta}\,\varepsilon
. This finishes the proof.
∎

A combination of Corollary
4.5
and Lemma
4.6
yields the following result.

Proposition 4.7
.

Take the same notations as in Corollary
4.5
. Then, for all
h,\varepsilon>0

(86)

Q(\tilde{f}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})+\widetilde{Q}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}({\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{\tilde{f}})+\frac{c_{d}}{p^{\prime}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|{\varrho_{\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}}^{p/2}-\varrho_{f}^{p/2}}\right|^{2}\leq\frac{h^{2}}{\varepsilon}\,\mathsf{M}+\varepsilon\,\mathsf{D}\,.

Here,
\mathsf{M}
and
\mathsf{D}
are defined in Proposition
4.1
, and
p=1+\frac{2}{d}
,
c_{d}=\omega_{d}^{-2/d}
.

We now use the previous result to get Lebesgue norms estimates. We recall
\Omega_{a,b}=\{x:\dist(x,\{V\leq a\})<b\}
.

Lemma 4.8
.

With the same notations as in Lemma
4.6
, Proposition
4.7
, Proposition
4.1
. Let
d\geq 3
. Then for all
0<\varepsilon\leq 1
and
q\in[1,2]

(87)

\left\|{\varrho_{f}-\varrho_{\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}}}\right\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\mathsf{L}_{q}\left(\frac{h^{2}}{\varepsilon}\mathsf{M}+\varepsilon\,\mathsf{D}\right)^{1/2}.

Additionally,

(88)

\displaystyle\left\|{f-\tilde{f}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\mathsf{L}_{1}+\mathsf{C}_{1}\right)\Big(\frac{h^{2}}{\varepsilon}\mathsf{M}+\varepsilon\,\mathsf{D}\Big)^{1/2}+\mathsf{C}_{2}\left(h+\varepsilon\right)

\displaystyle\left\|{\tilde{f}-f_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{f-\tilde{f}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}^{1/2}

where
\mathsf{C}_{1}
and
\mathsf{C}_{2}
are defined by equations (
77
) and (
78
).

Proof.

Notice that all the terms on the left-hand side of inequality (
86
) are positive, hence they are controlled individually by the sum of the terms on the right-hand side.

First we prove Inequality (
87
). Recall for any
a,b\geq 0
and
\theta\leq 1
, we have the following inequality
\left|{a-b}\right|\leq\frac{1}{\theta}\left|{a^{\theta}-b^{\theta}}\right|\left(a^{1-\theta}+b^{1-\theta}\right)
. Set
\theta=1-p
with
p=1+\frac{2}{d}
so that
1-\theta=\frac{d-2}{2d}>0
.
Then, we find thanks to Hölder’s inequality

\displaystyle\left\|{\varrho-\varrho_{\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}}}\right\|_{L^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{p}\left\|{\varrho_{f}^{p/2}-\varrho_{\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}}^{p/2}}\right\|_{L^{2}}\left(\left\|{\varrho_{f}^{\frac{d-2}{2d}}}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|{\varrho_{\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}}^{\frac{d-2}{2d}}}\right\|_{L^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\left\|{\varrho-\varrho_{\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}}}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{p}\left\|{\varrho_{f}^{p/2}-\varrho_{\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}}^{p/2}}\right\|_{L^{2}}\left(\left\|{\varrho_{f}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{d-2}{2d}}+\left\|{\varrho_{\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{d-2}{2d}}\right).

Observe that Proposition
4.7
readily gives the desired inequality for the
L^{2}
estimate. As for the
L^{1}
estimate, we note that for any
\varrho\geq 0
and
\delta>0
, if
r=\frac{2d}{d-2}
, so that
r^{\prime}=d
and
\frac{1}{r}+\frac{1}{r^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{2}
, then by Hölder’s inequality

\left\|{\varrho^{\frac{d-2}{2d}}}\right\|_{L^{2}}\leq\left\|{e^{-\delta\left|{x}\right|}}\right\|_{L^{r^{\prime}}}\left\|{e^{\delta\left|{x}\right|}\varrho^{\frac{d-2}{2d}}}\right\|_{L^{r}}=\left\|{e^{-\delta\left|{x}\right|}}\right\|_{L^{d}}\left\|{e^{\frac{2d\delta}{d-2}\left|{x}\right|}\varrho}\right\|_{L^{1}}^{\frac{d-2}{2d}}.

We now choose
\delta=\frac{d-2}{2d}\beta
, and use
\left\|{e^{\beta\left|{x}\right|}\varrho_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}}\right\|_{L^{p}}\leq\left\|{e^{\beta\left|{x}\right|}g_{1}}\right\|_{L^{1}}\left\|{e^{\beta\left|{x}\right|}\varrho_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}}\right\|_{L^{p}}
for
\varepsilon\leq 1
to finish the proof of the first estimates.

Secondly, we prove (
88
). To this end, we set
v:=c_{d}\,\varrho_{\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}}^{2/d}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{f}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}=\left|{\xi}\right|^{2}-v
and use the triangle inequality

(89)

\left\|{f-\widetilde{f}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{L^{1}}\leq\left\|{\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{f}\leq 0}-\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{f}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\leq 0}}\right\|_{L^{1}}+\left\|{\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{f}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\leq 0}-\widetilde{f}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{L^{1}}

where we used
f=\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{f}}
with
\mathcal{H}_{f}=\left|{\xi}\right|^{2}-c_{d}\,\varrho_{f}^{2/d}
. The first term on the right-hand side of Inequality (
89
) can be readily estimated with (
87
) with
q=1
via

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\left|{\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{f}\leq 0}-\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{f}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\leq 0}}\right|=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|{\varrho_{f}^{1/d}-\varrho_{\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}}^{1/d}}\right|^{d}\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|{\varrho_{f}-\varrho_{\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}}}\right|.

For the second term on the right-hand side of Inequality (
89
), we have

\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega_{1,1}^{c}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|{\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{f}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\leq 0}-\tilde{f}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right|\leq\int_{\Omega_{1,1}^{c}}\varrho_{\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}}\\
\leq\int_{\Omega_{1,1}^{c}\times B_{1/2}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(x-y)\,g_{\varepsilon}(y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y+\int_{\Omega_{1,1}^{c}\times B_{1/2}^{c}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(x-y)\,g_{\varepsilon}(y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\\
\leq\int_{\Omega_{1,1/2}^{c}\times B_{1/2}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(x)\,g_{\varepsilon}(y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y+\frac{M_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}{\varepsilon^{d/2}}\int_{B_{1/2}^{c}}\,e^{-\frac{\pi\left|{y}\right|^{2}}{\varepsilon}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\\
\leq\int_{\Omega_{1,1/2}^{c}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}+C_{d}^{\prime}\,M_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,\varepsilon

where
C_{d}^{\prime}=2\left(\frac{d+2}{2e\pi}\right)^{1+d/2}
. By Agmon’s estimates, and more precisely Inequality (
36
)

\int_{\Omega_{1,1/2}^{c}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\leq\frac{4}{3}\,e^{-\frac{1}{2\hbar}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,V_{-}\leq\frac{4h}{3\pi}M_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\left\|{V_{-}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}.

On the other hand, for any
R>0
, splitting the integral over the region where
\lvert\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{f}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\rvert<R
and
\lvert\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{f}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\rvert\geq R
yields

\int_{\Omega_{1,1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|{\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{f}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\leq 0}-\tilde{f}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right|\leq\int_{\Omega_{1,1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathds{1}_{\left|{\left|{\xi}\right|^{2}-v}\right|<R}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi+\frac{1}{R}\,Q(\tilde{f}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})\,.

Computing the first integral of this last expression gives

\int_{\Omega_{1,1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathds{1}_{\left|{\left|{\xi}\right|^{2}-v}\right|<R}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi=\omega_{d}\int_{\Omega_{1,1}}(v+R)_{+}^{d/2}-(v-R)_{+}^{d/2}\\
\leq d\,\omega_{d}\,R\int_{\Omega_{1,1}}v^{\frac{d}{2}-1}+\left|{\Omega_{1,1}}\right|\left(2R\right)^{d/2}.

Using Hölder’s inequality to control the integral of
v^{d/2-1}
by
M_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
, we finally get

(90)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\left|{\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{f}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\leq 0}-\tilde{f}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right|\leq M_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\left(C^{\prime}_{d}\varepsilon+\frac{8h}{3\pi}\left\|{V_{-}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}h\right)+\mathsf{C}_{1}\,R+\frac{1}{R}\,Q(\tilde{f}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})

with
\mathsf{C}_{1}=d\,\omega_{d}^{\frac{2}{d}}\left|{\Omega_{1,1}}\right|^{\frac{2}{d}}\,M_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{1-\frac{2}{d}}+\left|{\Omega_{1,1}}\right|2^{\frac{d}{2}}
. Finally choose
R=(\frac{h^{2}}{\varepsilon}\mathsf{M}+\varepsilon\,\mathsf{D})^{1/2}
. The desired estimate then follows from the bound of
Q(\tilde{f}_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}})
in Proposition (
4.7
), and the definition of
\mathsf{C}_{2}
.

For the last inequality we observe that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|{f-\tilde{f}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right|

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}f\left(1-\tilde{f}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)+\left(1-f\right)\tilde{f}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}f+\tilde{f}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-2\,f\,\tilde{f}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\tilde{f}+f_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-2\,\tilde{f}\,f_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=h^{d}\tr\!\left(\,\tilde{\!{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}_{f}+{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-2\,\,\tilde{\!{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}_{f}\,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right)

\displaystyle=h^{d}\tr\!\left(\left|{\,\tilde{\!{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}_{f}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right|^{2}+\,\tilde{\!{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}_{f}\left(\mathbf{1}-\,\tilde{\!{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}_{f}\right)\right)\geq\left\|{\tilde{f}-f_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

where in the third equality we used
\left\langle f,G*f_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right\rangle=\left\langle G*f,f_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\right\rangle
and
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}f=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}G*f
.
∎

It remains to remove the convolutions by
G_{\varepsilon}
using the regularity of
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
to obtain the proof of the main result of the section.

Proof of Proposition
4.1
.

First, since
\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in\dot{B}^{1/2}_{2,\infty}
we get from Inequality (
87
) for
q=2
, and the elementary bound
\sqrt{a+b}\leq\sqrt{a}+\sqrt{b}
,
that for some constant
C_{d}>0

\displaystyle\left\|{\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-\varrho_{f}}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\varrho_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}-\varrho_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|{\varrho_{f}-\varrho_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}}\right\|_{L^{2}}
